Bricks and τ \tau -tilting theory under base field extensions Thanks: E.D.B. gratefully acknowledges support from the French ANR grant CHARMS (ANR-19-CE40-0017-02), the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) – Project ID 281071066 – TRR 191, and from the NAWI Graz Postdoc fellowship. Thanks: M.K. acknowledges support from the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) – Project ID 281071066 – TRR 191. Thanks: E.J.H. is supported by an AMS-Simons travel grant.
Erlend D. Børve, Maximilian Kaipel

Abstract
. Let K : k K:k be a field extension and let Λ \Lambda be a finite-dimensional k k -algebra. We investigate the relationship between Λ \Lambda and Λ K ≔ Λ ⊗ k K \Lambda_{K}{\coloneqq}\Lambda\otimes_{k}K with particular emphasis on various aspects of τ \tau -tilting theory and bricks. We show that many types of objects for Λ \Lambda lift injectively to the same type of object for Λ K \Lambda_{K} , and many common constructions in τ \tau -tilting theory commute with the process of extending the base field. One of our main applications is the construction of a faithful functor from the τ \tau -cluster morphism category 𝔚 ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathfrak{W}(\Lambda) of Λ \Lambda to the τ \tau -cluster morphism category 𝔚 ⁡ ( Λ K ) \mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K}) of Λ K \Lambda_{K} . In particular, this establishes a faithful functor from 𝔚 ⁡ ( Λ ) \mathfrak{W}(\Lambda) to a group whenever k k is of characteristic zero which has many important consequences. In the appendix, E. J. Hanson shows the analogous result whenever k k is a finite field. Moreover, we give some nontrivial examples to illustrate the behaviour of τ \tau -tilting finiteness under base field extension.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
12F10, 16G10, 16G60, 18E40, 52A20, 55P20.

0.
Introduction

Let
K:k
be a field extension and fix a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra
\Lambda
. The endeavours of many people have aimed to understand the relationship between the representation theory of the finite-dimensional
k
-algebra
\Lambda
and that of the finite-dimensional
K
-algebra
\Lambda_{K}\coloneqq\Lambda\otimes_{k}K
. These investigations were initiated in
[
JL82
]
, where various homological dimensions of
\Lambda
, like the global dimension and the finitistic dimension, were compared with those of
\Lambda_{K}
. For this purpose, the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K:\mods\Lambda\to\mods\Lambda_{K}
, between the categories of finite-dimensional right modules, plays a central role. For many of the homological questions in
[
JL82
]
, the best behaviour is exhibited when
K:k
is MacLane separable, see
Definition

1.5
. With this assumption, the global dimensions of
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{K}
coincide.

Moreover, an important feature of an algebra is its representation type. The celebrated trichotomy theorem of
[
Dro77
]
states that a finite-dimensional algebra over an algebraically closed field is either representation finite, tame or wild. Over arbitrary base fields, the notion of tameness has to be adapted
[
CB91
,
CB92
]
. The relationship between the representation type of
\Lambda
and the representation type of
\Lambda_{K}
was first considered in
[
JL82
]
. It was shown that when
K:k
is MacLane separable, then
\Lambda
is representation finite if and only if
\Lambda_{K}
is representation finite. The goal of establishing a similar relationship for generalised representation tame algebras was pursued in
[
Kas01
,
MP13
,
Pér16
]
.

One important tool for investigating the category of finite-dimensional modules of
\Lambda
is Auslander–Reiten theory
[
AR75
]
. In
[
Kas00
]
, another proof was given of the fact that representation finiteness is preserved by MacLane separable field extension by relating the Auslander–Reiten theory of
\Lambda
with that of
\Lambda_{K}
.
In fact, representation finiteness of
\Lambda
is characterised by properties of the scalar extension functor when
k
is an infinite field
[
Zay94
]
. Moreover, different classes of algebras have been shown to be preserved and reflected under base field extensions in certain cases
[
Li21
,
Li23
]
.

Even if an investigation of the behaviour under base field extensions is not the primary objective of the research, it is often an essential tool, which enables more geometric insights into the objects of interest
[
DIJ19
,
IK24
]
. This last idea is also common in algebraic geometry, being applied in descent theory, see
[
Hoh23
,
Mil24
]
and the references therein. A particularly important case is the extension of Gabriel’s theorem beyond algebraically closed fields, where the setting of species replaces that of quivers
[
DR75
,
DR76
]
. The relationship between these two settings is deeply rooted in the theory of field extensions, see also
[
DD06
,
DD07
,
Hub04
]
. Moreover, the proof of the Second Brauer–Thrall Conjecture can be extended to perfect fields using the theory of base field extensions
[
BS05
]
.

The work in the present paper is both intrinsically as well as extrinsically motivated. We begin by studying the behaviour of many objects within representation theory under the scalar extension functor, but with the ultimate aim of gaining new insights into an important category of interest, namely the
\tau
-cluster morphism category
. The general setting of our work is
\tau
-tilting theory
[
AIR14
]
, which uses Auslander–Reiten theory to extend classical tilting theory.

0.1.
General results about
\tau
-tilting theory and bricks

The study of
\tau
-tilting theory has gained widespread popularity since its introduction in
[
AIR14
]
and has widely been accepted as the correct framework for generalising statements about hereditary algebras to arbitrary finite-dimensional algebras. In particular, it can be seen as a generalisation of (classical) tilting theory to higher projective dimensions, since whenever the
\tau
-tilting modules, the central objects of
\tau
-tilting theory, have projective dimension at most one, they coincide with (classical) tilting modules
[
ARS95
, Cor. IV.4.7]
.

As its name suggests,
\tau
-tilting theory makes use of the Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau
, whose behaviour under base field extension has already been studied in
[
Kas00
]
. Modules which do not have nonzero homomorphisms to their Auslander–Reiten translation are called
\tau
-rigid
, and give rise to
\tau
-perpendicular subcategories of
\mods\Lambda

[
Jas15
]
. Similar to perpendicular subcategories for hereditary algebras
[
GL91
]
, these are of utmost importance within
\tau
-tilting theory. Notably, they are equivalent to module categories of other finite-dimensional algebras. Closely related to this are

\tau
-exceptional sequences
[
BM21b
]
, which
generalise exceptional sequences over hereditary algebras
[
CB93
,
Rin94
]
to guarantee that sequences of maximal length always exist.

Moreover,
\tau
-tilting theory is closely related with the study of bricks, since the endotop of each
\tau
-rigid module decomposes into bricks
[
DIJ19
,
Asa21
]
. These indecomposable direct summands are
\Hom
-orthogonal, which makes them (left-finite) semibricks
[
Asa20
]
. It is a well-known result that semibricks correspond bijectively to wide subcategories
[
Rin76
, Thm. 1.2]
(recited in
Theorem

3.5
), and the strategy of lifting individual objects using the scalar extension functor appears to be the correct way to lift subcategories. Most of the terms in the following theorem have thus been explained. For the
remaining ones, we refer to later sections for precise definitions.

Theorem 0.1
.

(simplified)
Let
K:k
be a field extension and
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K:\mods\Lambda\to\mods\Lambda_{K}
induces injective maps on the following objects:

(1)

\tau
-rigid modules, which restricts to support
\tau
-tilting modules and tilting modules;

(2)

\tau
-rigid pairs, which restricts to support
\tau
-tilting pairs;

(3)

functorially finite torsion classes;

(4)

\tau
-perpendicular intervals of torsion classes;

(5)

complete signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences;

(6)

complete
\tau
-exceptional sequences;

(7)

left-finite semibricks;

(8)

right-finite semibricks.

If
k
is perfect, then the same holds for the following objects:

(9)

semibricks;

(10)

wide subcategories.

Moreover, the partial orders on (3), (4) and (10) are preserved.

Proof.

(1) and (2) are both a combination of
Lemma

2.4
and
Lemma

2.7
and
Corollary

2.8
; (3) is a combination of (2) and
[
AIR14
, Thm. 2.7]
, the fact that the poset structure is preserved is
Lemma

2.11
; (4) is
Proposition

2.24
, the fact that the poset structure is preserved follows from the additivity of
-\otimes_{k}K
; (5) is
Lemma

2.27
; (6) is
Corollary

2.31
; (7) and (8) follow from (2) and
[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]
and the fact that
-\otimes_{k}K
is injective-on-objects; (9) is
Proposition

3.4
; (10) is a combination of (9) and
[
Rin76
, Thm. 1.2]
(the latter result is recited in
Theorem

3.5
).
The fact that the poset structure is preserved follows from the exactness of
-\otimes_{k}K
.
∎

Besides individual objects and subcategories, it is important for our purpose to consider additional constructions and their interaction with base field extension. We briefly describe the terms appearing here. The inverse Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau^{-1}
gives rise to
\tau^{-1}
-tilting theory. Any support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting module can be constructed explicitly from a support
\tau
-tilting module, as we recall in
Proposition

2.15
. Moreover, each
\tau
-rigid module can be completed to a support
\tau
-tilting module. In the poset of support
\tau
-tilting modules there is a unique maximal completion and a unique minimal completion, which are known as the Bongartz and co-Bongartz completions, respectively
[
AIR14
]
. We have already discussed that taking the endotop of a
\tau
-rigid module gives a left-finite semibrick. The dual construction for
\tau^{-1}
-rigid modules gives right-finite semibricks
[
Asa20
]
. Finally, constructing tilted
[
HR82
]
and preprojective algebras
[
BGL87
]
from hereditary algebras is classical. We refer to later sections for precise statements of the claims in the following theorem.

Theorem 0.2
.

(simplified)
Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then the following constructions for
\Lambda
commute with scalar extension along
K:k
:

(1)

The bijection from
\tau
-rigid pairs to
\tau^{-1}
-rigid pairs;

(2)

Taking co-Bongartz completions of
\tau
-rigid pairs;

(3)

Taking Bongartz completions of
\tau
-rigid pairs.

If
K:k
is MacLane separable, then the following constructions also commute with taking base field extensions:

(4)

Turning
\tau
-rigid modules into left-finite semibricks;

(5)

Turning
\tau^{-1}
-rigid modules into right-finite semibricks;

(6)

Constructing the tilted algebra for a tilting module of a hereditary algebra.

(7)

Constructing the preprojective algebra for a projective module of a hereditary algebra.

Proof.

(1) is
Lemma

2.16
; (2) is
Lemma

2.22
; (3) is
Lemma

2.23
;
(4) is
Lemma

3.9
; (5) is
Lemma

3.10
; (6) is
Lemma

2.9
and
(7) is
Proposition

5.3
.
∎

Geometrically, the
\tau
-tilting theory of
\mods\Lambda
is encoded in the
g
-vector fan
\Sigma(\Lambda)

[
DIJ19
]
. The idea of considering a fan for such a purpose originates in the theory of cluster algebras
[
FZ07
]
, and its properties have been studied in various settings, see
[
AHI+22
,
AY23
]
and the references therein. Moreover, the
g
-vector fan embeds into the wall-and-chamber structure
[
Asa20
,
BST19
]
, which is the support of the stability scattering diagram
[
Bri17
]
. Since all stable modules are bricks, this relationship highlights another facet of the connection between bricks and
\tau
-tilting theory. In contrast to the
g
-vector fan, investigating the wall-and-chamber structure goes beyond the domain of
\tau
-tilting theory. Nonetheless, we are able to describe the behaviour of both under base field extensions.

Theorem 0.3
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then the
g
-vector fan
\Sigma(\Lambda)
embeds into the
g
-vector fan
\Sigma(\Lambda_{K})
. Moreover, if
K:k
is a finite and separable extension, then the wall-and-chamber structure of
\Lambda
embeds into the wall-and-chamber structure of
\Lambda_{K}
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is
Theorem

2.18
and the moreover part is
Theorem

2.21
.
∎

Wall-and-chamber structures are intrinsically linked with stability conditions
[
Kin94
,
KS08
,
Bri17
,
BPP16
]
. It has been studied how the stability manifold is altered by extension of scalars
[
Sos12
,
CQ24
]
. See also
[
CMQ24
]
for a similar result about quiver coverings and scattering diagrams.

0.2.
\tau
-cluster morphism category

Generally speaking, the
\tau
-cluster morphism category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
of a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
encompasses many of the aforementioned objects of the
\tau
-tilting theory of
\Lambda
. More specifically, its objects are
\tau
-perpendicular subcategories of
\mods\Lambda
and morphisms are given by
\tau
-rigid pairs (which represent corresponding
\tau
-tilting reductions
[
Jas15
]
). These categories were originally introduced for hereditary algebras, where they are closely connected to exceptional sequences, cluster algebras and maximal green sequences
[
IT17
,
IT21
]
. In these cases, the classifying space often forms a
K(\pi,1)
space for the picture group, which is of independent interest
[
ITW16
,
IT22
]
. Similar questions about arbitrary finite-dimensional algebras have received much attention
[
BH22
,
BH23
,
BM21a
,
HI21b
,
HI21a
,
Kai25
,
Kai24
,
Bør21
]
, but the understanding of this category is limited to a few special cases. Part of a sufficient condition for the classifying space to be a
K(\pi,1)
space is the existence of a faithful functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to G
for some group
G
viewed as a groupoid with one object
[
Igu14
]
(see
Proposition

4.6
). We apply the theory developed in this article to obtain new families of algebras for which the
\tau
-cluster morphism category admits a
faithful group functor
, that is, a faithful functor from
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
into a group.

Theorem 0.4
.

(
Theorem

4.12
)
Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. There exists a faithful functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
. Therefore, if
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
admits a faithful group functor, so does
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
.

This result establishes a well-behaved relationship between the
\tau
-cluster morphism categories of different algebras. Such a connection is quite special and only few other settings are known to admit such a relationship between
\tau
-cluster morphism categories of distinct algebras
[
BH22
,
Kai24
]
.

In concurrent work, the first-named author shows that
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor whenever the base field is algebraically closed and of characteristic 0
[
Bø
]
. Since any field extension of a field of characteristic 0 is MacLane separable (by definition), one can clearly use
Theorem

0.4
to remove the assumption of algebraic closure on
k
(see
Corollary

4.13
).

Corollary 0.5
.

(
Corollary

4.13
)
Let
k
be a field of characteristic 0 and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor.

With a similar goal in mind, it is shown in the appendix that the analogous result holds for algebras over finite fields.

Theorem 0.6

(Theorem
A.16
, simplified)
.

Let
k
be a finite field and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor.

The proof of Theorem
A.16
is based upon the theory of
picture groups
and
completed (finitary) Ringel–Hall algebras
, see Appendix
A
for the relevant definitions and historical context. There are also cases where these arguments can be lifted to infinite fields:

Corollary 0.7

(Corollary
A.17
, simplified)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra over an arbitrary field, and suppose there exists a lattice isomorphism
\eta:\tors\Lambda\rightarrow\tors\Lambda^{\prime}
for some finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda^{\prime}
over a finite field. Then
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor.

For example,
Corollary

0.7
implies that, over any field, the
\tau
-cluster morphism categories of the following classes of
k
-algebras admit faithful group functors: representation finite hereditary algebras (as already shown in
[
IT22
]
), preprojective algebras of representation finite hereditary algebras (
Proposition

A.19
), and representation finite string algebras (
Proposition

A.20
).

Moreover, if an algebra
\Lambda
defined over a finite field or a field of characteristic zero has at most three isomorphism classes of simple objects, then the results of
[
BH22
,
Igu14
]
imply that the classifying space
\mathcal{B}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is in fact a
K(\pi,1)
space.

0.3.
\tau
-tilting finiteness under base field extension

Inspired by representation finite algebras, the class of
\tau
-tilting finite algebras consists of algebras admitting only finitely many isomorphism classes of basic support
\tau
-tilting modules
[
DIJ19
]
. For hereditary algebras, these two notions coincide, but even representation wild algebras may be
\tau
-tilting finite in general. The importance of
\tau
-tilting finiteness is highlighted by the plethora of equivalent conditions to being
\tau
-tilting finite, see
[
AHMV19
,
DIJ19
,
Gar24
,
IRTT15
,
MŠ17
,
ST22
,
Sen23
]
. Given a Maclane separable field extension
K:k
and a
k
-algebra
\Lambda
, it turns out that
\Lambda
is representation finite if and only if
\Lambda_{K}
is, by
[
JL82
, Thm. 3.3]
, as recited in
Theorem

1.7
(
2
). Similar relationships for representation tame algebras have been investigated
[
Kas01
,
MP13
,
Pér16
]
, but generally more care needs to be taken because the definition of tameness relies on algebraic closure of the ground field as mentioned previously. In the setting of
\tau
-tilting theory one possible analogue of tameness has been proposed in
[
AY23
]
. An algebra
\Lambda
is
g
-tame
if its
g
-vector fan is dense in the ambient space. Tame algebras (naturally defined over algebraically closed fields) were shown to be
g
-tame in
[
PY23
]
.

It is a natural and important question to ask whether
\tau
-tilting finiteness behaves similarly with respect to base field extension. In this direction, we are able to show the following.

Theorem 0.8
.

There exists a
\tau
-tilting finite
k
-algebra
\Lambda
and a field extension
K:k
such that
\Lambda_{K}
is not
g
-tame and thus not
\tau
-tilting finite. On the other hand, let
K:k
be MacLane separable, and
\Pi(\Gamma)
the preprojective algebra of a representation finite hereditary
k
-algebra
\Gamma
. Then
\Pi(\Gamma_{K})\cong\Pi(\Gamma)\otimes_{k}K
. Thus, the generally representation infinite algebra
\Pi(\Gamma)
remains
\tau
-tilting finite under MacLane separable field extensions.

Proof.

Such an example is given in
Example

5.1
. The isomorphism
\Pi(\Gamma_{K})\cong\Pi(\Gamma)\otimes_{k}K
is shown in
Proposition

5.3
. By
Theorem

1.7
this means that
\Pi(\Gamma)\otimes_{k}K
is again a preprojective algebra of a representation finite hereditary
K
-algebra. The conclusion follows from
[
AHI+22
, Thm. 7.9]
which implies that preprojective algebras over representation finite hereditary algebras are
\tau
-tilting finite. This is because the poset of support
\tau
-tilting modules is isomorphic to a weak order on the Weyl group corresponding to the Dynkin type of the hereditary algebra, which is a finite group if the algebra is representation finite.
∎

We consider it an interesting problem to find further examples or conditions for representation infinite but
\tau
-tilting finite algebras to remain
\tau
-tilting finite under base field extension, see
Lemma

5.5
. See also
[
KKK+24
, Sec. 3.2]
for similar results about skew-group algebras.

Terland for useful discussions.

E.J.H. is thankful to E.D.B. and M.K. for encouraging him to write Appendix
A
, for including it in their paper, and for offering suggestions for improvement. He also thanks Kiyoshi Igusa and Hipolito Treffinger for useful conversations pertaining to Ringel–Hall algebras.

1.
Preliminaries on field extensions

Let
k
be a field. Let
\overline{k}
denote an algebraic closure of
k
. Recall that
\Lambda
will always denote a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Given a field extension
K:k
, denote by
\Lambda_{K}
the
K
-algebra
\Lambda\otimes_{k}K
. The
scalar extension functor
is then defined as:

\displaystyle-\otimes_{k}K:\mods\Lambda

\displaystyle\to\mods\Lambda_{K}

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto M_{K}\coloneqq M\otimes_{k}K.

Since the field extension
K
of
k
is a faithfully flat
k
-module, the scalar extension functor is an exact and faithful functor. Moreover, it admits a right adjoint (forgetful)
restriction functor

(-)|_{\Lambda}:\mods\Lambda_{K}\to\mods\Lambda
. We begin by collecting some essential properties of the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K
.

In the introduction, we defined
\mods\Lambda
to be the (abelian) category of finite-dimensional right
\Lambda
-modules. Throughout the entire text, let
\proj\Lambda
denote the additive subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
spanned by finite-dimensional projective
\Lambda
-modules. Given a finite-dimensional right
\Lambda
-module, let
\add(M)
denote the full subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
spanned by all direct summands of modules of the form
M^{m}
, for some
m\geq 1
(in particular, one could define
\proj\Lambda
as
\add(\Lambda_{\Lambda})
, where
{\Lambda_{\Lambda}}
denotes
\Lambda
considered as a right
\Lambda
-module).

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. Let
M,N\in\mods\Lambda
.

(1)

Suppose that
M
is indecomposable. Then
M
is a direct summand of
N
if and only if
M_{K}
and
N_{K}
have a common nonzero direct summand.

(2)

If
M
and
N
are indecomposable, then
M_{K}
and
N_{K}
share a common nonzero direct summand if and only if
M\simeq N
.

(3)

M\simeq N
if and only if
M_{K}\simeq N_{K}
. In other words, the functor
-\otimes_{k}K
is injective-on-objects up to isomorphism.

(4)

Let
P\in\proj\Lambda
. Then
P_{K}\in\proj\Lambda_{K}
. Moreover, each projective
\Lambda_{K}
-module arises as a direct summand of
P_{K}
for some
P\in\proj\Lambda
.

(5)

Suppose that
M
and
N
are basic. If
\add(M_{K})=\add(N_{K})
, then
M\simeq N
.

(6)

\bigcup_{X\in\add(M)\cap\add(N)}\add(X_{K})=\{X:X\in\add(M_{K})\cap\add(N_{K})\}
.

Proof.

(1)-(3) are
[
Kas00
, Lem. 2.5]
, where (2) is also known as the Noether–Deuring Theorem, see
[
Lam01
, Thm. 19.25]
. The asserion in (4) is taken from
[
Kas00
, Lem. 2.1]
.

(5) Let
X
be an indecomposable direct summand of
M
. From
\add(M_{K})=\add(N_{K})
we have that
X_{K}
is a direct summand of
(N_{K})^{r}
for some
r\geq 1
. By (1), it follows that
X
is a direct summand of
N^{r}
, and since
X
is indecomposable, we further deduce that
X
is a direct summand of
N
. Repeating this for all indecomposable direct summands of
M
, we obtain that every indecomposable direct summand of
M
is a direct summand of
N
. Reversing the argument, we find similarly that every indecomposable direct summand of
N
is a direct summand of
M
. It follows that
\add(M)=\add(N)
. Since
M
and
N
are basic, the isomorphism
M\simeq N
follows.

(6) To show the inclusion
(\subseteq)
, let
X\in\add(M)\cap\add(N)
be indecomposable. Then
X
is a direct summand of
M
and a direct summand of
N
. It follows from (1) that
X_{K}
is a direct summand of
M_{K}
and of
N_{K}
, hence
X_{K}\in\add(M_{K})\cap\add(N_{K})
. Thus
\add(X_{K})\subseteq\add(M_{K})\cap\add(N_{K})
, whence the inclusion
(\subseteq)
holds. Conversely, to show the inclusion
(\supseteq)
, let
Y\in\add(M_{K})\cap\add(N_{K})
be indecomposable. It is easy to see that
Y\in\add(Z_{K})\subseteq\add(M_{K})
and that
Y\in\add(Z_{K}^{\prime})\subseteq\add(N_{K})
, for some indecomposable modules
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mods\Lambda
. From (1) it follows that
Z\in\add(M)
and that
Z^{\prime}\in\add(M)
. It now follows from (2) that
Z\simeq Z^{\prime}
. In conclusion,
Y\in\add(Z_{K})
, where
Z\in\add(M)\cap\add(N)
, which is enough to show that the inclusion
(\supseteq)
also holds.
∎

Most importantly, the homomorphisms and extensions between two modules behave well.

Lemma 1.2
.

[
Kas00
, Lem. 2.2]

Let
X,Y\in\mods\Lambda
and
i
a nonnegative integer. Then the canonical homomorphism of
K
-vector spaces

\Ext^{i}_{\Lambda}(X,Y)\otimes_{k}K\to\Ext^{i}_{\Lambda_{K}}(X_{K},Y_{K})

is an isomorphism which is natural in both arguments.

The remainder of this section recalls different types fields and field extensions which we frequently use throughout this paper. Standard notions of Galois theory will be presumed well-known to the reader. Recall that a field extension
K:k
is a
Galois extension
if it is separable, normal and algebraic. If
K:k
is a finite, we denote its degree by
[K:k]
.

Definition-Proposition 1.3
.

A field
k
is said to be perfect if the following equivalent assertions hold:

(1)

The field extension
\overline{k}:k
is separable, where
\overline{k}
denotes an algebraic closure of
k
.

(2)

Every algebraic field extension of
k
is separable.

(3)

Every (possibly nonalgebraic) field extension
k
is separable.

Proof.

It is obvious that (3)
\implies
(2)
\implies
(1). Also, since every algebraic extension of
k
is intermediate to
\overline{k}:k
, one easily proves that (1)
\implies
(2), whence the first two assertions are equivalent. To complete the proof, we refer to
[
Bou03
, Thm. V.15.5.3(b)]
, where it is shown that (2)
\implies
(3).
∎

For example, by
[
Jac76
, Thm. IV.3]
, all fields of characteristic zero and all finite fields are perfect. It is obvious from the definition that algebraically closed fields are perfect.

Remark 1.4
.

Let
K:k
be a finite field extension. There is a functorial isomorphism

(M_{K})|_{\Lambda}\simeq M^{[K:k]}.

Consequently, for any indecomposable direct summand
M_{i}\in\add(M_{K})
we have
M_{i}|_{\Lambda}\simeq M^{r}
for some
r\leq[K:k]
. Consider for instance the Galois extension
\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}
which satisfies
[\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}]=2
, and consider
\mathbb{R}
as a 1-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. It is then clear that
{(\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C})}|_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Of particular importance is the following slightly less well-known type of field extension.

Definition 1.5
.

Given a field extension
L:k
, let
L_{1}\colon k
and
L_{2}\colon k
be intermediate field extensions of
L:k
. We say that
L_{1}\colon k
and
L_{2}\colon k
are
linearly disjoint over
k
in
L
if the
k
-homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}\otimes_{k}L_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}L_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}\otimes\lambda_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in}

is an isomorphism of
k
-algebras, where
L_{1}L_{2}
is the smallest subalgebra of
L
containing
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
.
A field extension
K:k
is said to be
MacLane separable
if either
\charac(k)=0
or
\charac(k)>p\neq 0
and the field extensions
K
and
k^{p^{-1}}
are linearly disjoint over
k
in
\overline{K}
. Here
k^{p^{-1}}
denotes the subfield
\{\lambda\in{\overline{K}}:\lambda^{p}\in k\}
of
{\overline{K}}
.

For example, an algebraic field extension is separable if and only if it is MacLane separable
[
Jac76
, Thm. IV.9]
. In particular, a finite field extension is MacLane separable if and only if it is separable. Moreover, every separable field extension is MacLane separable
[
Jac76
, p. 163, MacLane’s Criterion]
, but the converse is not true in general, see
Example

1.6
below.

Example 1.6
.

There are examples of MacLane separable field extensions that are not separable. By the proceeding discussion, the larger field must be infinite and of prime characteristic. One can construct a general class of examples by fixing a field
k
of characteristic
p\neq 0
and letting
K=k(\xi,\xi^{p^{-1}},\xi^{p^{-2}},\dots)
, where
\xi
is transcendental over
k
. Then
K:k
is MacLane separable but not separable
[
Jac76
, Exercise IV.5.1]
. The field
K
is indeed infinite and of characteristic
p
. It happens to also be perfect, since every element in
K
is a
p
th power.

Theorem 1.7
.

[
JL82
, Thms. 2.4 and 3.3]

Let
K:k
be a field extension.

(1)

The field extension
K:k
is MacLane separable if and only if scalar extension along
K:k
preserves the global dimension of all finite-dimensional
k
-algebras, which is to say that
the equality
\gldim\Lambda=\gldim\Lambda_{K}
holds for all finite-dimensional
k
-algebras
\Lambda
.

(2)

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. If
K:k
is MacLane separable, then
\Lambda
is representation finite if and only if
\Lambda_{K}
is representation finite.

(3)

In the setting of (
2
) above, every indecomposable
\Lambda_{K}
-module is a direct summand of a
\Lambda_{K}
-module
M_{K}
for some indecomposable
M\in\mods\Lambda
.

In light of the assertions in
Theorem

1.7
, one may argue that MacLane separability interacts well with homological algebra, as can be seen by many of the other results in
[
JL82
]
.
In
Section

5
, we explore the behaviour of
\tau
-tilting finiteness, where the analogous statement of
Theorem

1.7
(
2
) fails to hold. Further properties in relation to the radical and the socle of a module are summarised in the following lemma.

Lemma 1.8
.

[
Kas00
, Lem. 3.3, 3.5]

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Let
K:k
be a field extension and
M\in\mods\Lambda
.

(1)

There is an inclusion
(\rad\Lambda)_{K}\subseteq\rad\Lambda_{K}
.

(2)

There is an inclusion
(\rad M)_{K}\subseteq\rad M_{K}
.

(3)

There is an inclusion
(\soc M)_{K}\subseteq\rad M_{K}
.

If
K:k
is MacLane separable, then all of the above are equalities.

2.
\tau
-tilting theory

The area of
\tau
-tilting theory
[
AIR14
]
can be seen as a generalisation of classical tilting theory to modules of arbitrary projective dimensions, alternatively a completion of the theory with respect to mutation. As the name suggests, the Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau
plays a central role. In the work of
[
Kas00
]
, the behaviour of almost split exact sequences of Auslander–Reiten theory under base field extension was studied. In particular, the Auslander–Reiten translations of
\mods\Lambda
and
\mods\Lambda_{K}
, denoted by
\tau_{\Lambda}
and
\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}
, respectively, are connected. We will make this statement precise in
Lemma

2.2
, where we also include a proof for the convenience of the reader. First, we establish a similar result for the Nakayama functors
\nu_{\Lambda}:\mods\Lambda\to\mods\Lambda
and
\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}:\mods\Lambda_{K}\to\mods\Lambda_{K}
, on which our proof of
Lemma

2.2
will depend.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
M\in\mods\Lambda
. There is an isomorphism of
\Lambda_{K}
-modules
(\nu_{\Lambda}M)_{K}\simeq\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}
.

Proof.

The result follows from applying
Lemma

1.2
twice to obtain the isomorphisms and using the definition
\nu_{?}(-)=\Hom_{k}(\Hom_{?}(-,?),k)
. Indeed, the proof is then as follows:

\displaystyle\nu_{\Lambda}(M)\otimes_{k}K

\displaystyle=\Hom_{k}(\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,\Lambda),k)\otimes_{k}K

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{K}(\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,\Lambda)\otimes_{k}K,K)

\displaystyle\simeq\Hom_{K}(\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K},\Lambda_{K}),K)

\displaystyle=\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Similarly, we establish a relationship between the Auslander–Reiten translations.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
Kas00
, Cor 3.6]

Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
M\in\mods\Lambda
. There is an isomorphism of
\Lambda_{K}
-modules
(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K}\simeq\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}
.

Proof.

Let
P^{1}\xrightarrow{p^{1}}P^{0}\xrightarrow{p^{0}}M\to 0
be a minimal projective presentation of
M
in
\mods\Lambda
. By
Lemma

1.1
(4),
P_{K}^{1}
and
P_{K}^{0}
are projective
\Lambda_{K}
-modules, and by
Lemma

1.8
(2) the exact sequence
P_{K}^{1}\xrightarrow{p_{K}^{1}}P_{K}^{0}\xrightarrow{p_{K}^{0}}M_{K}\to 0
is a minimal projective presentation of
M_{K}
in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. After applying the (exact) Nakayama functor to these sequences, consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu_{\Lambda}P^{0})_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu_{\Lambda}P^{1})_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu_{\Lambda}M)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The three maps on the right are isomorphisms by
Lemma

2.1
, and since we have applied Nakayama functors to minimal projective presentations, the relevant squares commute. By exactness and the Five Lemma, there is an induced isomorphism
\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}\simeq(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K}
, as required.
∎

We now introduce the central objects of
\tau
-tilting theory.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
M\in\mods\Lambda
and
P\in\proj\Lambda
.

(1)

We say
M
is
\tau
-rigid
if
\Hom(M,\tau_{\Lambda}M)=0
. If additionally
|M|=|\Lambda|
then we say
M
is
\tau
-tilting
.

(2)

We say that the pair
(M,P)
is
\tau
-rigid
if
M
is a
\tau
-rigid
\Lambda
-module,
P
is a projective
\Lambda
-module and
\Hom(P,M)=0
. If additionally
|M|+|P|=|\Lambda|
then we say
(M,P)
is a
support
\tau
-tilting pair
. In this case, the
\Lambda
-module
M
is called
support
\tau
-tilting
.

(3)

We say that
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite
if it only admits finitely many isomorphism classes of indecomposable
\tau
-rigid modules. Otherwise it is said to be
\tau
-tilting infinite
.

We denote by
\operatorname{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigid}\Lambda
the collection of basic
\tau
-rigid
\Lambda
-modules and by
\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda
the collection of basic
\tau
-rigid pairs
(M,P)
of
\mods\Lambda
, that is, such that
M
and
P
are basic. We denote the set of basic support
\tau
-tilting pairs by
\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
.
The following result extends
[
DIJ19
, Prop. 6.6(d)]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension, let
M\in\mods\Lambda
and let
P\in\proj\Lambda
. Then
M
is
\tau
-rigid if and only if
M_{K}
is
\tau
-rigid. Moreover,
(M,P)
is a
\tau
-rigid pair if and only if
(M_{K},P_{K})
is a
\tau
-rigid pair.

Proof.

The first statement follows directly from the sequence of isomorphisms

\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,\tau_{\Lambda}M)\otimes_{k}K\simeq\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K},(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K})\simeq\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K},\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K})

where the first isomorphism is
Lemma

1.2
and the second follows from
Lemma

2.2
. Moreover,
Lemma

1.2
similarly implies that
\Hom_{\Lambda}(P,M)=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K},M_{K})
, which gives the desired result.
∎

Since the number of indecomposable direct summands of modules may be difficult to control under field extension, it is necessary to take a more homological approach to understand (support)
\tau
-tilting modules and support
\tau
-tilting pairs.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
denote the bounded homotopy category of
\proj\Lambda
, and for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
[i]
denote the
i
th power of the suspension functor in
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
.
Let
P^{\bullet}\in K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
.

(1)

We say that
P^{\bullet}
is
presilting
if
\Hom(P^{\bullet},P^{\bullet}[i])=0
for all
i>0
.

(2)

We say that
P^{\bullet}
is
silting
if it is presilting and
\thick(P^{\bullet})=K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
.

Finally,
P^{\bullet}
is called
2-term
if it is isomorphic to an object
(P^{i},d^{i})
in
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
such that
P^{i}=0
for
i\neq-1,0
.

Denote the collection of basic 2-term presilting and basic silting objects of
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
by
2\mathchar 45\relax\operatorname{presilt}\Lambda
and
2\mathchar 45\relax\operatorname{silt}\Lambda
respectively. The derived scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}^{\mathbf{L}}K:K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)\to K^{b}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
behaves nicely with respect to presilting and silting complexes.

Lemma 2.6
.

(see also
[
IK24
, §2.4]
)
Let
K:k
be a field extension. If
P^{\bullet}\in K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
is a presilting object, then
P_{K}^{\bullet}\in K^{b}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
is a presilting object. Moreover, if
P^{\bullet}
is a silting object, then
P_{K}^{\bullet}
is a silting object.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows directly from the adaptation of
Lemma

1.2
to this setting, where
\Ext^{i}(-,-)
becomes
\Hom(-,-[i])
. If
P^{\bullet}\in K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
is silting, then
\Lambda\in\thick(P^{\bullet})\subseteq K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
. Since
-\otimes_{k}^{\mathbf{L}}K
is a triangle functor, it follows that
\Lambda_{K}\in\thick(P_{K}^{\bullet})\subseteq K^{b}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
which implies that
P_{K}^{\bullet}
is silting in
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
.
∎

The study of 2-term (pre)silting objects is the main focus of
[
DF15
]
. By
[
AIR14
, Thm. 3.2]
the connection between 2-term (pre)silting objects and
\tau
-rigid pairs is given by the following mutually inverse maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathchar 45\relax\operatorname{silt}\Lambda{,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

where
F(M,P)=(P^{1}\oplus P\xrightarrow{(f\hskip 8.19447pt0)}P^{0})
and
P_{1}\xrightarrow{f}P_{0}\to M
is a minimal projective presentation of
M
,

and
G(P^{\bullet})=(H^{0}(P),(P_{1})^{\prime\prime})
, where we decompose
P^{\bullet}=P^{1}\xrightarrow{d}P^{0}
as
P^{\bullet}=(P^{1})^{\prime}\oplus(P^{1})^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{(d^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt0)}P^{0}
with
d^{\prime}
right minimal, see
[
AIR14
, Prop. 3.6(b)]
.
This relationship commutes with taking field extensions.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. If
(M,P)
is a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
, then
(M_{K},P_{K})
is a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. In particular, if
M
is a
\tau
-tilting module in
\mods\Lambda
, then
M_{K}
is a
\tau
-tilting module in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
.

Proof.

Let
P^{1}\xrightarrow{d}P^{0}\to M\to 0
be a minimal projective presentation of
M
. By
Lemma

1.8
(2),
P_{K}^{1}\xrightarrow{d_{K}}P_{K}^{0}\to M_{K}\to 0
is a minimal projective presentation of
M_{K}
. Since
(M,P)
is
\tau
-tilting,
[
AIR14
, Thm. 3.2]
states that the corresponding 2-term complex
P^{1}\oplus P\xrightarrow{(d\hskip 8.19447pt0)}P^{0}
is a silting object of
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda)
. By
Lemma

2.6
, the complex
P_{K}^{1}\oplus P_{K}\xrightarrow{(d_{K}\hskip 8.19447pt0)}P_{K}^{0}
is a silting object in
K^{b}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
. Moreover, its corresponding
\tau
-tilting pair is clearly
(M_{K},P_{K})
, completing the proof.
∎

As a corollary of the above, we obtain a similar result for classical tilting modules. Recall that
M\in\mods\Lambda
is a
tilting module
if
\projdim_{\Lambda}M\leq 1
,
\Ext_{\Lambda}^{1}(M,M)=0
and
|M|=|\Lambda|
.

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
M\in\mods\Lambda
be a tilting
\Lambda
-module. Then
M_{K}\in\mods\Lambda_{K}
is a tilting
\Lambda_{K}
-module.

Proof.

By
[
ARS95
, Cor. IV.4.7]
, if
\projdim_{\Lambda}M\leq 1
, then
\Ext_{\Lambda}^{1}(M,M)=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,\tau M)=0
. Consequently, tilting modules are exactly
\tau
-tilting modules of projective dimension at most 1. By
[
JL82
, Prop. 2.1(i)]
the
\Lambda_{K}
-module
M_{K}
is such that

\projdim_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}=\projdim_{\Lambda}M\leq 1.

Together with
Lemma

2.7
, this means that
M_{K}
is a
\tau
-tilting
\Lambda_{K}
-module of projective dimension at most 1, and hence tilting as required.
∎

Recall that a
k
-algebra
\Lambda
is called
tilted
provided that there is a hereditary
k
-algebra
\Gamma
and a tilting
\Gamma
-module
T\in\mods\Gamma
such that
\Lambda\simeq\End_{\Gamma}(T)^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
. Much of the representation theory of tilted algebras can be understood by understanding the original hereditary algebra. We refer to
[
Bon81
,
HR82
,
AHHK07
]
for thorough background on tilting theory.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension. If
\Lambda
is a tilted
k
-algebra, then
\Lambda_{K}
is a tilted
K
-algebra.

Proof.

Assume that
\Lambda\simeq\End_{\Gamma}(T)^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}
for some hereditary
k
-algebra
\Gamma
and some tilting
\Gamma
-module
T\in\mods\Gamma
. Then

\displaystyle\Lambda_{K}

\displaystyle\simeq(\End_{\Gamma}(T)^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}})_{K}

\displaystyle\simeq((\End_{\Gamma}(T))_{K})^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}

(by
[
Kas00
, Lem. 2.3]
)

\displaystyle\simeq\End_{\Gamma_{K}}(T_{K})^{{}^{\mathrm{op}}}

(by
Lemma

1.2
)

Since
\Gamma
is hereditary, it follows that
\Gamma_{K}
is hereditary since its global dimension is preserved by
Theorem

1.7
(
1
). Moreover, by
Corollary

2.8
,
T_{K}
is a tilting
\Gamma_{K}
-module, whence
\Lambda_{K}
is a tilted
K
-algebra as required.
∎

Recall that a
torsion class

\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
is a full subcategory which is closed under factor modules and extensions
[
Dic66
]
. Dually, a
torsion-free class

\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
is a full subcategory which is closed under submodules and extensions. Denote the set of torsion classes (resp. torsion-free classes) of
\mods\Lambda
by
\tors\Lambda
(resp.
\torf\Lambda
). In fact,
\tors\Lambda
and
\torf\Lambda
form lattices under inclusion. For a full subcategory
\mathcal{X}
of
\mods\Lambda
, we define its
right Hom-orthogonal
by

\mathcal{X}^{\perp}\coloneqq\{Y\in\mods\Lambda\colon\Hom_{\Lambda}(X,Y)=0\quad\forall X\in\mathcal{X}\}.

Taking the Hom-orthogonal
(-)^{\perp}
provides an anti-isomorphism from the lattice
\tors\Lambda
to the lattice
\torf\Lambda
. We define the
left Hom-orthogonal

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{X}
dually. For a module
M\in\mods\Lambda
, define the subcategories
M^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}M
of
\mods\Lambda
as
(\add M)^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}(\add M)
, respectively.

There is an important subset of
functorially finite
torsion classes (resp. torsion-free classes), denoted by
\ftors\Lambda\subseteq\tors\Lambda
(resp.
\ftorf\Lambda\subseteq\torf\Lambda
). We recall the definition here. An arbitrary additive subcategory
\mathcal{X}
of
\mods\Lambda
is
contravariantly finite
(resp
covariantly finite
) if every
M\in\mods\Lambda
admits a
\Lambda
-homomorphism
X\xrightarrow{\beta}M
(resp.
M\xrightarrow{\alpha}X
) with the (nonuniversal) property that the induced map
\Hom_{\Lambda}(X{{}^{\prime}},\beta)
(resp.
\Hom_{\Lambda}(\alpha,X{{}^{\prime}})
) is surjective for all
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. We call the
\Lambda
-homomorphism
\beta
(resp.
\alpha
) a
right
\mathcal{X}
-approximation
(resp.
left
\mathcal{X}
-approximation
) of
M
. A
functorially finite
subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
is by definition a full subcategory that is both contravariantly and covariantly finite.

For a
\Lambda
-module
M
, let
\Fac M
denote the smallest full subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
containing
M
which is closed under factor modules and extensions. Dually, let
\Sub M
denote the smallest full subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
containing
M
which is closed under submodules and extensions.

Theorem 2.10
.

[
AIR14
, Thm. 2.7]

The map

\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\longrightarrow\ftors\Lambda

which maps
(M,P)\in\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
to
\Fac M
, is a bijection. Its inverse maps
\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
to the unique support
\tau
-tilting pair
(\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{T}),P)
, where
\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{T})
is the largest basic
\Ext
-projective
\Lambda
-module in
\mathcal{T}
, which is defined by

\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{T})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\in\ind^{\prime}\Lambda\\
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda}({X},\mathcal{T})=0\end{subarray}}X,

where
\ind^{\prime}\Lambda
denotes a set of indecomposable
\Lambda
-modules, in which exactly one representative of each isomorphism class occurs.

This relationship with torsion classes endows the set of support
\tau
-tilting pairs with a partial order given by
(M,P)\leq(N,Q)
whenever
\Fac M\subseteq\Fac N
. In fact, this partial order can also be seen intrinsically via a process of mutation, see
[
AIR14
, Sec. 2.3]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension and let
M
and
N
be
\tau
-rigid
\Lambda
-modules such that
\Fac M\subseteq\Fac N\subseteq\mods\Lambda
. Then
\Fac M_{K}\subseteq\Fac N_{K}\subseteq\mods\Lambda_{K}
.

Proof.

Take
X\in\Fac M_{K}
. By definition, there is an epimorphism
M_{K}^{r}\twoheadrightarrow X\to 0
in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
for some
r\geq 1
. Since
\Fac M\subseteq\Fac N\subseteq\mods\Lambda
, it follows that in particular
M^{r}\in\Fac N
which gives rise to an epimorphism
N^{s}\twoheadrightarrow M^{r}\to 0
in
\mods\Lambda
for some
s\geq 1
. Applying the (exact) scalar extension functor then gives an epimorphism
N_{K}^{s}\twoheadrightarrow M_{K}^{r}\to 0
in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. In conclusion, there is a chain of epimorphisms
N_{K}^{s}\twoheadrightarrow M_{K}^{r}\twoheadrightarrow X
, which yields
X\in\Fac N_{K}
as required.
∎

Consequently, we obtain the following relationship, which is to be compared with
[
IK24
, Thm. 2.14(a)]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. For a
\Lambda_{K}
-module
N
, let
N^{b}
denote a (choice of) basic direct summand of
N
with
\add N=\add N^{b}
.
We have an embedding of posets

\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\to\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda_{K}

sending
(M,P)
to
(M^{b}_{K},P^{b}_{K})
. This map is well-defined up to isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
-\otimes_{k}K
is injective-on-objects by
Lemma

1.1
(3), it follows from
Lemma

2.7
and
Lemma

2.11
that it can be regarded to send support
\tau
-tilting pairs to support
\tau
-tilting pairs. This is all we need to show.
∎

Similarly, it is possible to obtain the following result concerning functorially finite torsion classes.

Corollary 2.13
.

We have an embedding of posets

\ftors\Lambda\to\ftors\Lambda_{K}

sending
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}_{K}\coloneqq\Fac M_{K}
, where
(M,P)
is a support
\tau
-tilting pair such that
\mathcal{T}=\Fac M
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{T}=\Fac N
for some
\tau
-rigid module
N
, then
\mathcal{T}_{K}=\Fac N_{K}
.

Proof.

The map
\ftors\Lambda\to\ftors\Lambda_{K}
is defined as follows:

(2.a)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M_{K}^{b},P_{K}^{b})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{T}_{K}.}}
Theorem

2.10
bijection
Proposition

2.12
injection
Theorem

2.10
bijection

All maps in
Eq.

2.a
are order-preserving. By the results cited along the arrows in (
2.a
), it follows that this is indeed a well-defined embedding. Let
M
be
\tau
-rigid and assume
\mathcal{T}=\Fac M
. Consider the image of
\mathcal{T}
under the inverse of the map in
Theorem

2.10
, and denote it by
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
. It is clear from the construction that
\Fac M=\Fac M^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

2.11
, we have
\Fac M_{K}=\Fac M_{K}^{\prime}
, and therefore
\mathcal{T}_{K}=\Fac M_{K}^{\prime}=\Fac M_{K}
, as required.
∎

We remark that
\mathcal{T}_{K}
is usually not equal to the full subcategory of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
obtained by applying
-\otimes_{k}K
to every object of
\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
. This fact illustrates why it is often preferable to study the behaviour of individual objects under base field extension, rather than the behaviour of subcategories.

2.1.
\tau^{-1}
-tilting theory

Dual to
\tau
-tilting theory, which uses the Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau
, there is
\tau^{-1}
-tilting theory using the inverse AR-translation
\tau^{-1}
. We include some explicit results in this subsection for later reference.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
M\in\mods\Lambda
and let
Q\in\inj\Lambda
.

(1)

We say
M
is
\tau^{-1}
-rigid
if
\Hom_{\Lambda}(\tau^{-1}_{\Lambda}M,M)=0
. If additionally
|M|=|\Lambda|
then we say
M
is
\tau^{-1}
-tilting
.

(2)

We say that the pair
(M,Q)
is
\tau^{-1}
-rigid
if
M
is a
\tau^{-1}
-rigid
\Lambda
-module,
Q
is an injective
\Lambda
-module and
\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,Q)=0
. If additionally
|M|+|Q|=|\Lambda|
then we say that
(M,Q)
is
support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting
. The
\Lambda
-module
M
is a
support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting
\Lambda
-module
.

Denote by
\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda
the collection of basic support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pairs for
\Lambda
. It is clear that the dual statements of
Lemma

2.4
and
Lemma

2.7
hold for
\tau^{-1}
-rigid and support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pairs.

Proposition 2.15
.

[
AIR14
, Discussion following Thm. 2.15]

We have a bijection

(2.b)

H:\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\to\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda

given by
H(M,P)=(\tau M\oplus\nu P,\nu M_{\mathrm{pr}})
, where
M_{\mathrm{pr}}
is the largest projective direct summand of
M
. It fits into a commutative square

(2.c)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ftors\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ftorf\Lambda.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fac}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sub}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\perp}}

The interaction between the bijection in
Proposition

2.15
and the extension of scalars is described in the following.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. The following square is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda_{K}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{k}K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{k}K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

Proof.

Let
(M,P)\in\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
. By
Lemma

2.2
, there is an isomorphism
(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K}\simeq\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}
and by
Lemma

2.1
, there is an isomorphism
(\nu_{\Lambda}P)_{K}\simeq\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K}
. Combining these two observations, there is an isomorphism
(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)_{K}\simeq\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}\oplus\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K}
as the tensor functor is additive. Finally,
Lemma

1.1
(4) implies that
(M_{\mathrm{pr}})_{K}\simeq(M_{K})_{\mathrm{pr}}
and using
Lemma

2.1
again implies the desired result.
∎

2.2.
g
-vector fans and wall-and-chamber structures

Many aspects of
\tau
-tilting theory of an algebra
\Lambda
are encoded geometrically in its
g
-vector fan
\Sigma(\Lambda)

[
DIJ19
]
. Consider the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)
of the full subcategory
\proj\Lambda\subseteq\mods\Lambda
, whose basis is given by the isomorphism classes of indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-modules. The
g
-vector fan then lives the
real Grothendieck group

K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}=K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{|\Lambda|}
, which is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Let
P^{-1}\to P^{0}\to M
be the minimal projective presentation of
M
. The
g
-vector
of
M
is defined to be

g^{M}=[P^{0}]-[P^{-1}]\in K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}.

The
g
-vector cone
of
(M,P)
is the nonnegative linear span

\mathcal{C}_{(M,P)}=\spann_{\geq 0}\{g^{M_{1}},\dots,g^{M_{t}},-g^{P_{t+1}},\dots,-g^{P_{r}}\}\subseteq K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}},

where
M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}M_{i}
and
P\simeq\bigoplus_{i=t+1}^{r}P_{i}
are decompositions of
M
and
P
into indecomposable direct summands, respectively. Finally, the
g
-vector fan

\Sigma(\Lambda)
is given by the collection of
g
-vector cones

\Sigma(\Lambda)=\{\mathcal{C}_{(M,P)}:(M,P)\in\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda\}.

By considering containment of cones, we may regard the
g
-vector fan as a poset. As we have demonstrated in the previous sections, the
\tau
-tilting theory of
\Lambda
maps
nicely into the
\tau
-tilting theory of
\Lambda_{K}
for any field extension
K:k
. The following result establishes such a relationship for the respective
g
-vector fans. In
Theorem

2.18
, we suppress the subscript
\mathbb{R}
and denote the real Grothendieck group simply by
K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)
.

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. Denote by
K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}\subseteq K_{0}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
the image of the embedding
-\otimes_{k}K:K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)\hookrightarrow K_{0}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
given by
[P]\mapsto[P_{K}]
.
There is an injective map of fans (as posets)

(2.d)

\displaystyle\Sigma(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\Sigma(\Lambda_{K})

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(M,P)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}.

which restricts to a (geometric) embedding of fans

(2.e)

\displaystyle\Sigma(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\Sigma(\Lambda_{K})\cap K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(M,P)}\ni v

\displaystyle\mapsto v\otimes_{k}K\in\mathcal{C}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}.

Proof.

Since the
g
-vector fan
\Sigma(\Lambda)
is completely determined by basic
\tau
-rigid pairs, we can restrict to those, see
[
Kai25
, Lem. 6.12]
.

Let
(M_{1},P_{1})
and
(M_{2},P_{2})
be basic
\tau
-rigid pairs of
\mods\Lambda
. From
Lemma

2.4
, it follows that
((M_{1})_{K},(P_{1})_{K})
and
((M_{2})_{K},(P_{2})_{K})
are
\tau
-rigid pairs of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. Therefore
\mathcal{C}_{((M_{1})_{K},(P_{1})_{K})},\mathcal{C}_{(M_{2})_{K},(P_{2})_{K})}\in\Sigma(\Lambda_{K})
, rendering our map of fans well-defined. Assume for a contradiction that
\mathcal{C}_{((M_{1})_{K},(P_{1})_{K})}=\mathcal{C}_{((M_{2})_{K},(P_{2})_{K})}
. By
[
DIJ19
, Cor. 6.7(2)]
, see also
[
Kai25
, Lem. 6.12, Thm. 6.13]
, we have that
\add((M_{1})_{K})=\add((M_{2})_{K})
and
\add((P_{1})_{K})=\add((P_{2})_{K})
. By
Lemma

1.1
(5)
M_{1}\simeq M_{2}
and
P_{1}\simeq P_{2}
as required, hence the map in
Eq.

2.d
is well-defined and injective.

Since
K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}
is a subspace of
K_{0}(\proj\Lambda_{K})
it is clear that
\Sigma(\Lambda_{K})\cap K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}
is again a fan. Now, let
(M,P)
be a basic
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Take
v\in\mathcal{C}_{(M,P)}
, then by definition we may write

v=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}g^{M_{i}}-\sum_{i=l}^{r}\alpha_{i}g^{P_{i}}

for
|M|=l
and
|M|+|P|=r
and some
0\leq\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. From
[
DIJ19
, Prop. 6.6(c)]
we know that
g^{(M_{K})}=(g^{M})\otimes_{k}K\in K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}
, from which we obtain

v\otimes_{k}K=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}g^{(M_{i})_{K}}-\sum_{i=l}^{r}\alpha_{i}g^{(P_{i})_{K}}.

It follows immediately that

v\otimes_{k}K\in\mathcal{C}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}\cap K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)_{K}.

From
Lemma

1.1
and
[
DIJ19
, Cor. 6.7(2)]
, see also
[
Kai25
, Thm. 6.13]
, it follows that the embedding is compatible with the fan structure, that is, intersections of cones are preserved.
∎

One important feature of the
g
-vector fan is that it embeds into the stability scattering diagram of
[
Bri17
]
, and into its support, the so-called wall-and-chamber structure
[
Asa20
,
BST19
]
. The wall-and-chamber structure itself captures stability phenomena of modules as described shortly.
Through the Euler form
\langle-,?\rangle:K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)\times K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)\to\mathbb{Z}
we may view
\theta\in K_{0}(\proj\Lambda)
as an element of
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)^{*}
via
\theta([M])=\langle\theta,[M]\rangle
, see
[
Asa21
]
. This establishes the connection between the
g
-vector fan and the following stability conditions.

Definition 2.19
.

[
Kin94
]

Let
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
denote the dual
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\theta\in K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
. We say that
M\in\mods\Lambda
is
\theta
-semistable
(resp.
\theta
-stable
) if
\theta([M])=0
and
\theta([L])\leq 0
(resp.
\theta([L])<0
) for all nonzero proper submodules
L
of
M
.

Thus, each
g
-vector
\theta
gives rise to a stability condition whose
\theta
-semistable objects are studied in
[
Asa21
,
BST19
,
Yur18
]
. Since
-\otimes_{k}K
and
(-)|_{\Lambda}
are both exact, they induce maps between Grothendieck groups and their duals

(2.f)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mods\Lambda_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mods\Lambda_{K})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)|_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\otimes_{k}K)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{k}K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}}

where for
\theta\in K_{0}(\mods\Lambda_{K})^{*}
we define
\theta_{K}^{*}\coloneqq(\theta\otimes_{k}K)^{*}
by
\theta_{K}^{*}([M])=\theta([M_{K}])
, and similarly we set
\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}([M])=\theta([M|_{\Lambda}])
. This alone is not enough to establish a well behaved relationship. However, recall from
Remark

1.4
that when
K:k
is a finite Galois extension, we have
(M_{K})|_{\Lambda}\simeq M^{[K:k]}
. Using this observation we are able to prove the following.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
K:k
be a finite and separable. Let
M\in\mods\Lambda
and let
\theta\in K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
. Then
M
is
\theta
-semistable if and only if
M_{K}
is
\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}
-semistable.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Assume
M
is
\theta
-semistable, then we obtain

\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}([M_{K}])=\theta([(M_{K})|_{\Lambda}])=\theta([M^{[K:k]}])=[K:k]\cdot\theta([M])=0.

Now let
L^{\prime}
be a submodule of
M_{K}
.
Since
K:k
is separable and algebraic, it follows from
[
Kas00
, Prop. 4.13]
that
L^{\prime}
is a direct summand of
L_{K}
for some
L\in\mods\Lambda
. Moreover, as
(-)|_{\Lambda}
is exact, we have that
L
is a submodule of
M
. We then get

\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}([L^{\prime}])=\theta([(L^{\prime})|_{\Lambda}])=\theta([L^{r}])=r\theta([L])\leq 0,

where
1\leq r\leq[K:k]
.

(\Leftarrow)
Assume
M_{K}
is
\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}
-semistable. Then we have

\theta([M])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta([M^{[K:k]}])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta([(M_{K})|_{\Lambda}])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}\theta([M_{K}])=0.

Entirely analogously we have for a submodule
L
of
M
that

\theta([L])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta([L^{[K:k]}])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta([(L_{K})|_{\Lambda}])=[K:k]^{-1}\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}\theta([L_{K}])\leq 0,

since
L_{K}
is a submodule of
M_{K}
as
-\otimes_{k}K
is exact.
∎

Now the stability spaces fit together nicely to form the
wall-and-chamber structure
. More precisely, given a nonzero module
M\in\mods\Lambda
, define its stability space of
M
to be

\Theta_{M}=\{\theta\in K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}:M\text{ is $\theta$-semistable}\},

which we call the
wall
associated to
M
. The collection of all walls defines the
wall-and-chamber structure
, where a chamber is a part of
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
which does not lie in the closure of any wall.

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
K:k
be a finite and separable extension. Then the wall-and-chamber structure of
\mods\Lambda
embeds into that of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
via the map
(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}
of
Eq.

2.f
.

Proof.

Lemma

2.20
states that the image of every wall in
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)^{*}
under
(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}
lies inside a wall of
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda_{K})^{*}
. To see that there are no other walls in the image of
(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}
, consider
M\in\mods\Lambda_{K}
such that
M
is
\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}
-semistable for some
\theta\in K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
. Since
K:k
is separable and algebraic, we know that
M\in\add N_{K}
for some
N\in\mods\Lambda
by
[
Kas00
, Prop. 4.13]
. We conclude the proof by showing that
N_{K}
is also
\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}
-semistable, hence any wall in the image of
(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}
lies inside the image of a wall of
K_{0}(\mods\Lambda)
under
(-)|_{\Lambda}^{*}
. We have

\displaystyle\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}([N_{K}])

\displaystyle=\theta([(N_{K})|_{\Lambda}])

\displaystyle=\theta([N^{[K:k]}])

\displaystyle=[K:k]\theta([N])

\displaystyle=\frac{[K:k]}{r}\theta([N^{r}])

\displaystyle=\frac{[K:k]}{r}\theta([M|\Lambda])

\displaystyle=\frac{[K:k]}{r}\theta|_{\Lambda}^{*}([M])

\displaystyle=0,

where
M|_{\Lambda}\simeq N^{r}
for some
1\leq r\leq[K:k]
.
∎

Comparing
Theorem

2.18
with
Theorem

2.21
shows that the
g
-vector fan, so in some sense the
\tau
-tilting theory, behaves well for all field extensions. Outside of the
g
-vector fan, the stability spaces may behave worse. Only with the additional assumption of having a finite and separable extension is it possible to gain control outside the
g
-vector fan.

2.3.
\tau
-perpendicular intervals

An interval
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda
is called
\tau
-perpendicular
if
\mathcal{U}=\Fac M
and
\mathcal{T}={}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}
for some
\tau
-rigid pair
(M,P)
. In this case, we also denote this interval by
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
. Via the bijection
Theorem

2.10
, the torsion class
\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)}
corresponds with the so-called
co-Bongartz completion

(M^{-},P^{-})\in\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
and the torsion class
\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}
corresponds with the so-called
Bongartz completion

(M^{+},P)\in\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
, see also
[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.4]
(the second component of the Bongartz completion stays invariant when passing from
(M,P)
to its Bongartz completion
(M^{+},P)
). By analogy, an interval of
\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
is called
\tau
-perpendicular if it is of the form
[(M^{-},P^{-}),(M^{+},P)]\subseteq\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
for some
\tau
-rigid pair
(M,P)
. We begin with the following observation, which is similar to
Lemma

2.11
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Taking co-Bongartz completions commutes with base field extension. More precisely, given
a field extension
K:k
and
(M,P)\in\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda
, then

((M_{K})^{-},(P_{K})^{-})=((M^{-})_{K},(P^{-})_{K}).

Proof.

By definition, the co-Bongartz completion of
(M,P)
is the uniquely determined basic support
\tau
-tilting pair
(M^{-},P^{-})
with the property that
\Fac(M^{-})=\Fac(M)
.
By
Lemma

2.4
,
(M_{K},P_{K})
is a
\tau
-rigid module in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
and thus
((M_{K})^{-},(P_{K})^{-})
is similarly defined as the unique basic
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
such that
\Fac((M_{K})^{-})=\Fac(M_{K})
. However, from
Corollary

2.13
we know that
\Fac(M^{-})=\Fac(M)
implies
\Fac((M^{-})_{K})=\Fac(M_{K})
. This implies that
\Fac((M_{K})^{-})=\Fac(M_{K})=\Fac((M^{-})_{K})
. Finally, since
(M^{-},P^{-})
is a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
, it follows that
((M^{-})_{K},(P^{-})_{K})
is a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
by
Lemma

2.7
. Thus,
Theorem

2.10
implies that
((M_{K})^{-},(P_{K})^{-})=((M^{-})_{K},(P^{-})_{K})
.
∎

With a little more work, we are also able to obtain a dual statement.

Lemma 2.23
.

Taking Bongartz completions commutes with base field extension. More precisely, let
K:k
be a field extension and
(M,P)\in\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda
. Then

((M_{K})^{+},P_{K})=((M^{+})_{K},P_{K}).

Proof.

Recall from
Proposition

2.15
that
H(M,P)
is defined by
(\tau M\oplus\nu P,M_{\textnormal{pr}})
. By definition, the Bongartz completion of
(M,P)
is the uniquely determined basic support
\tau
-tilting pair
(M^{+},P)
in
\mods\Lambda
with the property that
\Fac(M^{+})={{}^{\perp}\tau_{\Lambda}M}\cap P^{\perp}
.
Since
{{}^{\perp}\tau_{\Lambda}M}\cap P^{\perp}={{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)}
, this is equivalent to saying
\Fac(M^{+})={}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)
, and thus equivalent to
\Fac(M^{+})^{\perp}=\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)
since
\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P
is
\tau^{-1}
-rigid in
\mods\Lambda
. Because torsion classes uniquely determine their corresponding torsion-free classes, we equivalently have that
(M^{+},P)
is the unique
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
such that
\Fac(M^{+})^{\perp}=\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)
. It clearly follows that
\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda}M\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)=\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda}(M^{+})\oplus\nu_{\Lambda}P)
, so
Lemma

2.16
and the dual of
Corollary

2.13
imply that

(2.g)

\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}\oplus\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K})=\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}(M^{+})_{K}\oplus\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K}).

By
Lemma

2.4
,
(M_{K},P_{K})
is a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
, whence
((M_{K})^{+},P_{K})
is the unique basic
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
such that
\Fac((M_{K})^{+})^{\perp}=\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}\oplus\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K})
.
However, since
H(M^{+},P)
is a
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
, the dual of
Lemma

2.7
implies that
(H(M^{+},P))_{K}
is a
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
. By
Eq.

2.g
, it also corresponds to the torsion-free class
\Sub(\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}\oplus\nu_{\Lambda_{K}}P_{K})
under the dual of
Theorem

2.10
. This implies
(H(M^{+},P))_{K}=H((M_{K})^{+},P_{K})
. By
Lemma

2.16
we thus have

H((M^{+})_{K},P_{K})=(H(M^{+},P))_{K}=H((M_{K})^{+},P_{K}),

so that applying
H^{-1}
to both sides yields
((M^{+})_{K},P_{K})=((M_{K})^{+},P_{K})
as required.
∎

It is important to remark that the intervals
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda
and
[(M^{-},P^{-}),(M^{+},P)]
are not generally isomorphic as lattices because there may exist torsion classes in the interval which are not functorially finite. Nonetheless, there is a bijection between
\tau
-perpendicular intervals of
\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
and
\tau
-perpendicular intervals of
\tors\Lambda
. Denote by
\tauint(\tors\Lambda)
the collection of
\tau
-perpendicular intervals of
\tors A
and view it as a poset such that
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]\leq[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}]
whenever
[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
.

Proposition 2.24
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. There is a well-defined map

(2.h)

-\otimes_{k}K:\tauint(\tors\Lambda)\to\tauint(\tors\Lambda_{K})

given component-wise by
-\otimes_{k}K
. More precisely, let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Then applying the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K
component-wise to the two boundary
\tau
-tilting pairs in
\Lambda
of the
\tau
-perpendicular interval
[(M^{-},P^{-}),(M^{+},P)]\subseteq\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
gives rise to the
\tau
-perpendicular interval
[(M_{K}^{-},P_{K}^{-}),(M_{K}^{+},P_{K})]\subseteq\tors\Lambda_{K}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

2.22
,
Lemma

2.23
and the fact that the order of support
\tau
-tilting pairs are preserved by
Lemma

2.11
.
∎

2.4.
Ordered modules and
\tau
-exceptional sequences

Core techniques of
\tau
-tilting theory were applied in
[
BM21b
]
to introduce
\tau
-exceptional sequences. These sequences generalise exceptional sequences of bricks over hereditary algebras
[
CB93
,
Rin94
]
. Among other things, they are motivated by the fact that complete sequences of maximal length always exist. Since their introduction,
\tau
-exceptional sequences have received much attention
[
BH24
,
BHM24
,
BHM25
,
BKT25
,
BM23
,
HT24
,
Msa22
,
MT20
,
Non25b
,
Non25a
,
Ter25
]
.

Definition 2.25
.

[
BM21b
, Def. 1.3]

Let
\ind\Lambda
denote the subcategory of
\Lambda
spanned by indecomposable
\Lambda
-modules.
Denote by
C(\Lambda)
the disjoint union of two copies of
\ind\Lambda
. To distinguish the two copies, we consider objects in the first category to be “
unsigned
,” or just
\Lambda
-modules in their own right, and the objects of the second category to be (formally)
signed
. We denote a signed
\Lambda
-module by
\overline{M}
, where
M\in\mods\Lambda
. A sequence of objects in
C(\Lambda)
given by

(M_{1},M_{2},\dots,M_{t})

is called a
signed
\tau
-exceptional sequence
of length
t
in
\mods\Lambda
if:

(1)

M_{t}
is an unsigned
\Lambda
-module and
\tau
-rigid in
\mods\Lambda
, or
M_{t}
is a signed
\Lambda
-module whose underlying
\Lambda
-module is a projective object in
\mods\Lambda
; and

(2)

if
t>1
, then
(M_{1},\dots,M_{t-1})
is a
\tau
-exceptional sequence of length
t-1
in the abelian subcategory
M_{t}^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}\tau M_{t}{\subseteq\mods\Lambda}
, see also
Theorem

4.1
.

Such a sequence is called a
(n unsigned)
\tau
-exceptional sequence
if each
M_{i}
is an unsigned
\Lambda
-module. It is called a
signed
\tau
-exceptional sequence
otherwise.
A signed or unsigned
\tau
-exceptional sequences is
complete
if
t=|\Lambda|
.

In view of (2) above, let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Recall that
M^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}
is equivalent to
\mods\Gamma
for some finite-dimensional algebra
\Gamma
so that it has its own (relative) Auslander–Reiten translation and projective objects
[
Jas15
,
DIR+23
]
, see also
Theorem

4.1
. As a consequence, the unsigned
\Lambda
-modules
M_{1},\dots,M_{t-1}
in
Definition

2.25
are not necessarily
\tau
-rigid in
\mods\Lambda
or signed projective
\Lambda
-modules.

In some cases it is preferable to not use the recursive definition above and instead apply the following theorem which helps to index the signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences. Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair. An
ordered decomposition
of
(M,P)
is given by an ordered direct sum
X_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus X_{r}
, where each
X_{i}
is a direct summand of
M
or a signed direct summand of
P
, such that the underlying module is isomorphic to
M\oplus P
.
We say that the ordered decomposition is an
ordered decomposition into indecomposables
if each
X_{i}
is either an indecomposable
\Lambda
-module or an signed direct summand
\overline{P}_{i}
of
\overline{P}
, where
P_{i}
is indecomposable.

Theorem 2.26
.

[
BM21b
, Thm. 1.4]

There is a bijection

\left\{\minipage{204.44336pt}\begin{center}ordered decompositions of basic $\tau$-rigid pairs into indecomposables \end{center}\endminipage\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\minipage{153.33252pt}\begin{center}signed $\tau$-exceptional sequences \end{center}\endminipage\right\}

which restricts to support
\tau
-tilting pairs and complete signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences.

The idea of working with ordered decompositions instead of (signed)
\tau
-exceptional sequences was applied in
[
BKT25
]
to gain new insights into the mutation of signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences as introduced in
[
BHM24
]
. Moreover, it allows us to lift signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences under base field extension.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension. Then there is an injective map

\left\{\minipage{153.33252pt}\begin{center}complete signed $\tau$-exceptional sequences {in $\mods\Lambda$} \end{center}\endminipage\right\}\hookrightarrow\left\{\minipage{153.33252pt}\begin{center}complete signed $\tau$-exceptional sequences {in $\mods\Lambda_{K}$} \end{center}\endminipage\right\}

which factors through ordered decompositions of basic support
\tau
-tilting pairs.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.12
, there is an injective map from basic
\tau
-tilting pairs to basic
\tau
-tilting pairs in
\mods\Lambda
. Since
-\otimes_{k}K
is additive, any ordered decomposition of a
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
gives rise to an ordered decomposition of the resulting
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
into direct summands (which are not necessarily indecomposable). Fixing any choice of ordered decomposition for each such decomposable direct summand and combining this choice with
Theorem

2.26
yields the desired injective map.
∎

In fact, it is possible to restrict the bijection in
Theorem

2.26
to (unsigned)
\tau
-exceptional sequences. This was shown in the work of
[
MT20
]
, where a close relationship between
\tau
-exceptional sequences and stratifying systems
[
ES03
]
was established. We generalise their ideas, following
[
Han25
]
.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
M\in\mods\Lambda
be a basic
\Lambda
-module and consider an ordered decomposition
M\simeq M_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus M_{r}
.

(1)

We call
M_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus M_{t}
a
weakly TF-preordered decomposition
if for every
1\leq i\leq t
and every indecomposable direct summand
M^{\prime}
of
M_{i}
we have
M^{\prime}\not\in\Fac\left(\bigoplus_{j=i+1}^{t}M_{j}\right)
.

(2)

A weakly TF-preordered decomposition in which all direct summands are indecomposable is called
TF-ordered
.

The importance of TF-ordered decompositions becomes apparent in the following result.

Theorem 2.29
.

[
MT20
, Thm. 5.1]

The bijection in
Theorem

2.26
restricts to a bijection between TF-ordered
\tau
-rigid modules and (unsigned)
\tau
-exceptional sequences.

In contrast to signed
\tau
-exceptional sequences, the additional (TF-ordered) structure that (unsigned)
\tau
-exceptional sequences require makes it more difficult to understand their behaviour under base field extension. In particular, it seems necessary to impose stronger assumptions on the field extension.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
K:k
be a finite extension and let
M\simeq M_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus M_{r}
be a TF-ordered decomposition of a basic module
M\in\mods\Lambda
. Moreover, let
(M_{i})_{K}^{b}
denote a (choice of) basic direct summand of
(M_{i})_{K}
such that
\add((M_{i})_{K})=\add((M_{i})_{K}^{b})
for all
1\leq i\leq r
. Then:

(1)

(M_{1})_{K}\oplus\dots\oplus(M_{r})_{K}
is a weakly TF-preordered decomposition;

(2)

There exists exists an ordered decomposition
(M_{i})_{K}^{b}\cong\widehat{M}_{i,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{M}_{i,n_{i}}
into indecomposables for each
1\leq i\leq r
such that

\widehat{M}_{1,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{M}_{1,n_{1}}\oplus\widehat{M}_{2,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{M}_{r-1,n_{r-1}}\oplus\widehat{M}_{r,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{M}_{r,n_{r}}

is a TF-ordered decomposition of
M_{K}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to consider the case where
M\oplus N
is a TF-ordered decomposition of a basic
\Lambda
-module. The general case follows in the same way since the scalar extension and scalar restriction functors are additive. Assume
M\oplus N
is TF-ordered, that is,
M\not\in\Fac N
.

(1)

Assume that an indecomposable
\Lambda_{K}
-module
M^{\prime}\in\add(M_{K})
is generated by
N_{K}
. By definition this means that there is an exact sequence
N_{K}^{r}\to M^{\prime}\to 0

in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
which gets mapped to the exact sequence
N^{[K:k]\cdot r}\to M^{s}\to 0

by the exact restriction functor, as a result of
Remark

1.4
. This implies that
M\in\Fac N
, a contradiction.

(2)

Let
M^{\prime}
be an indecomposable direct summand of
M_{K}^{b}
. By (1), we have that
M^{\prime}\not\in\Fac(N_{K})
. Consider the torsion pair
(\Fac(N_{K}),(N_{K})^{\perp})
of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. Recall that it induces the
torsion-free functor

f_{N_{K}}
from
\mods\Lambda_{K}
into the torsion-free class
(N_{K})^{\perp}
. Since
M_{K}^{b}\not\in\Fac(N_{K})
the module
f_{N_{K}}(M^{\prime})
is a non-zero
\Lambda_{K}
-module. By
[
BM21b
, Prop. 5.6]
, the module
f_{N_{K}}(M^{\prime})
is
\tau
-rigid in
(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})\subseteq\mods\Lambda_{K}
.

Let
M_{K}^{b}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}(M_{K})_{j}
be a decomposition of
M_{K}^{b}
into pairwise nonisomorphic indecomposable direct summands. By
[
MT20
, Prop. 3.2]
, the module

\bigoplus_{j=1}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}f_{N_{K}}((M_{K})_{j}),

which is
\tau
-rigid in the abelian subcategory
(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})
of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
but may fail to be
\tau
-rigid in
\mods\Lambda
, admits a TF-ordered decomposition

(2.i)

f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{|M_{K}^{b}|}),

in the subcategory
(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})
of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. That is, there exists a decomposition such that

f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{\ell})\not\in\Fac_{(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})}\left(\bigoplus_{j>\ell}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{j})\right)

for each
1\leq\ell<|M_{K}^{b}|
. It is important to highlight that
\Fac_{(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})}\left(\bigoplus_{j>\ell}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{j})\right)
is a torsion class of the abelian subcategory
(N_{K})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\tau_{\Lambda}N_{K})
of
\mods\Lambda
but may fail to be a torsion class of
\mods\Lambda
.

By
[
MT20
, Prop. 3.2]
, the
\tau
-rigid module
N_{K}^{b}
of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
similarly admits a TF-ordered decomposition
N_{K}^{b}\cong\widehat{N}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{N}_{|N_{K}^{b}|}
in
\mods\Lambda
. We now claim that

(2.j)

\widehat{M}_{1}\oplus\dots\widehat{M}_{|M_{K}^{b}|}\oplus\widehat{N}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\widehat{N}_{|N_{K}^{b}|}

is a TF-ordered decomposition of
M_{K}^{b}\oplus N_{K}^{b}
in
\mods\Lambda
. Indeed,
\widehat{N}_{i}\not\in\Fac\left(\bigoplus_{j>i}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}\widehat{N}_{j}\right)
for all
1\leq i\leq|M_{K}^{b}|
by construction. Moreover, if

\widehat{M}_{i}\in\Fac\left(\widehat{M}_{i+1}\oplus\dots\widehat{M}_{|M_{K}^{b}|}\oplus N_{K}\right)

held, then there exists an epimorphism

\left(\widehat{M}_{i+1}\oplus\dots\widehat{M}_{|M_{K}^{b}|}\oplus N_{K}\right)^{s}\to\widehat{M}_{i}\to 0.

However, applying the additive
torsion-free functor
f_{N_{K}}
gives the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\widehat{M}_{i+1}\oplus\dots\widehat{M}_{|M_{K}^{b}|}\oplus N_{K}\right)^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{M}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigoplus_{j=i+1}^{|M_{K}^{b}|}f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{j})^{s}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{N_{K}}(\widehat{M}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{N_{K}}(h_{1})}

Note that
f_{N_{K}}(h_{1})
is a nonzero epimorphism, since
f_{N_{K}}(h_{1})\circ g_{1}=g_{2}\circ h_{1}
and
g_{2}\circ h_{1}
is a nonzero epimorphism. This yields a contradiction to
Eq.

2.i
being TF-ordered. Therefore, the decomposition of
Eq.

2.j
is a TF-ordered decomposition of
M_{K}^{b}\oplus N_{K}^{b}
in
\mods\Lambda
.

∎

Corollary 2.31
.

Let
K:k
be a finite field extension. Then there is an injective map

\left\{\minipage{153.33252pt}\begin{center}complete $\tau$-exceptional sequences in $\mods\Lambda$ \end{center}\endminipage\right\}\hookrightarrow\left\{\minipage{153.33252pt}\begin{center}complete $\tau$-exceptional sequences in $\mods\Lambda_{K}$ \end{center}\endminipage\right\}

which factors through TF-ordered decompositions of basic
\tau
-tilting modules.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.12
, there is an injective map from basic
\tau
-tilting modules in
\mods\Lambda
to basic
\tau
-tilting modules in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. By
Proposition

2.30
, any TF-ordered decomposition of a
\tau
-tilting module in
\mods\Lambda
gives rise to an weakly TF-preordered decomposition of the resulting
\tau
-tilting module in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
into direct summands (which are not necessarily indecomposable). By
Proposition

2.30
, there exists at least one possible choice of refining the TF-preordered decomposition.
∎

3.
Bricks and semibricks

In this section we are interested in a certain class of indecomposable modules called bricks. Formally, a module
M\in\mods\Lambda
is called a
brick
if its endomorphism
k
-algebra
\End_{\Lambda}(M)
is a division
k
-algebra. A collection of pairwise
\Hom
-orthogonal bricks is called a
semibrick
. In particular, (semi)simple modules are particular examples of (semi)bricks.
Denote by
\brick\Lambda
(resp.
\sbrick\Lambda
) the set of bricks (resp. semibricks) in
\mods\Lambda
.

The following lemma gives a sufficient condition for a brick to remain indecomposable after applying the scalar extension functor.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a (not necessarily finite-dimensional)
k
-algebra and let
M
