be a finite-dimensional
{\Gamma}
-module. If
M
is such that
\End_{{\Gamma}}(M)\simeq k
, then for any field extension
K:k
, we have
\End_{{\Gamma}_{K}}(M_{K})\simeq K
and hence
M_{K}
is indecomposable.

Proof.

The result essentially follows from the well-known fact that if
\dim_{k}\End_{{\Gamma}}(M)=1
then
M
is indecomposable. This is easy to see using the contrapositive: Given a nontrivial decomposition
M\simeq M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
of
M
, then

\dim_{k}\End_{{\Gamma}}(M)\geq\dim_{k}\End_{{\Gamma}}(M_{1})+\dim_{k}\End_{\Lambda}(M_{2})\geq 1+1=2.

Hence if
\End_{{\Gamma}}(M)\simeq k
, then it follows from
Lemma

1.2
that
\End_{{{\Gamma}}_{K}}(M_{K})\simeq K
(see
[
Lam01
, Lem. 7.4]
for infinite-dimensional algebras). In other words,
\dim_{K}\End_{{{\Gamma}}_{K}}(M_{K})=1
, hence
M_{K}
is indecomposable.
∎

An example of a field extension
K:k
such that
-\otimes_{k}K
preserves indecomposability is also considered in
[
JL82
, Thm. 3.5]
. As an almost trivial first property, we show that it is sometimes possible to lift semisimple modules.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Consider a (semi)simple
\Lambda
-module
S
. Then
S_{K}
is a semisimple
\Lambda_{K}
-module.

Proof.

We recall that a module
M
is semisimple if and only if its radical
\rad M
is zero. By
Lemma

1.8
(2), we have
0=(\rad S)\otimes_{k}K\simeq\rad S_{K}
, whence
S_{K}
is also semisimple.
∎

The following example occurs frequently in the literature, and it shows that the assumption of MacLane separability is necessary in
Lemma

3.2
.

Example 3.3

(see
[
Jac76
]
,
[
Kas00
, Rmk. 3.4]
)
.

Let
k
denote the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}(t)
of rational functions over the Galois field with two elements. Consider the non(Maclane )separable finite field extension
K:k
, where
K=k(\sqrt[2]{t})
, and let
\Lambda
denote
K
regarded as a
k
-algebra. Since
\Lambda
is also a field, it is a simple module over itself. Consequently,

\Lambda\otimes_{k}K=k[X]/(X-\sqrt[2]{t})^{2}\otimes_{k}K=K[X]/(X-\sqrt[2]{t})^{2}.

As a result, the simple
\Lambda
-module
\Lambda
does not remain semisimple when extending the scalars to
K
; the endomorphism algebra of
\Lambda\otimes_{k}K
as a
\Lambda\otimes_{k}K
-module contains a nilpotent element, namely
X-\sqrt[2]{t}
.

Given a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
, denote by
\ind(\mathcal{C})
the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable direct summands of modules
M
in
\mathcal{C}
. Now, consider the following generalisation of
Lemma

3.2
to all bricks. Due to its increased generality it requires a stronger assumption on the base field.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
k
be a perfect field and let
K:k
be a field extension.
Then the extension of scalars functor induces an injective map from bricks in
\mods\Lambda
to semibricks in
\mods\Lambda_{K}
which gives an injective map:

\ind(-\otimes_{k}K):\sbrick\Lambda\to\sbrick\Lambda_{K}

Moreover, if
B\in\brick\Lambda
satisfies
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\simeq k
, then
B_{K}\in\brick\Lambda_{K}
.

Proof.

The moreover part follows immediately from
Lemma

3.1
.

It is sufficient to show that
\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)
defines an injective map
\brick\Lambda\hookrightarrow\sbrick\Lambda_{K}
. Indeed,
\Hom
-orthogonality between different isomorphism classes of modules in a semibrick in
\mods\Lambda
is preserved by
Lemma

1.2
. Therefore, take
B\in\brick\Lambda
. By definition, we have that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)
is a division
k
-algebra. In particular, it is reduced, meaning that there are no nonzero nilpotent elements. Since
k
is perfect, it follows from
[
Bou03
, Thm. V.15.5.3(d)]
that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K\simeq\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(B_{K})
is a reduced
K
-algebra.

By
1.3
(3), the field extension
K:k
is separable, and thus also MacLane separable. By
Theorem

1.7
(
1
), the global dimension of the
K
-algebra
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
is thus the same as that of the
k
-algebra
\End_{\Lambda}(B)
. Since the latter is
0
, we deduce that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
is also semisimple.
Then, the Wedderburn–Artin Theorem implies that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
is isomorphic to a product of matrix rings of division
k
-algebras. If it were not the case that all of these matrix rings are division
k
-algebras, then
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
would not be reduced, contradicting the previous paragraph. Hence, we have that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
is a product of division
k
-algebras.

Consider a decomposition
B_{K}\simeq B_{1}\oplus\dots B_{m}
into indecomposable
\Lambda_{K}
-modules, and assume
f:B_{i}\to B_{j}
is a nonzero homomorphism for some
1\leq i,j\leq m
. Let
\widetilde{f}
denote the endomorphism of
B_{K}
induced by
f
.
Then,
\widetilde{f}
must be nilpotent, contradicting the fact that
\End_{\Lambda}(B)\otimes_{k}K
is reduced. It follows that
\ind(\add(B_{K}))
is a semibrick. We conclude that the map

\ind(-\otimes_{k}K):\sbrick\Lambda\to\sbrick\Lambda_{K}

is well-defined. It is injective since it is the restriction of the map

\ind(-\otimes_{k}K):\mods\Lambda\to\mods\Lambda_{K},

which was shown to be injective in
Lemma

1.1
(2) and
Lemma

1.1
(3).
∎

Example

3.3
illustrates that the assumption of
K:k
to be perfect is necessary, since the nilpotent endomorphism cannot be an isomorphism, whence
\Lambda\otimes_{k}K
is not a brick. We conclude with showcasing the importance of semibricks via their connection to wide subcategories.

Recall that a full subcategory
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
is called
wide
if it is closed under extensions, kernels and cokernels. Denote the collection of all wide subcategories of
\mods\Lambda
by
\wide\Lambda
.

Theorem 3.5
.

[
Rin76
, Thm. 1.2]

We have mutually-inverse maps defined as follows:

\displaystyle\sbrick\Lambda

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\wide\Lambda

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Filt_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle\{S:S\textnormal{ is simple in $\mathcal{W}$}\}

\displaystyle\mapsfrom\mathcal{W}

As a consequence, the bijection with semibricks allows us to lift wide subcategories under base field extension.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
k
be a perfect field and let
K:k
be a field extension. Then there exists an injective map

\wide\Lambda\hookrightarrow\wide\Lambda_{K}

given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{K}.}
Theorem

3.5
bijective
Proposition

3.4
injective
Theorem

3.5
bijective

It should be noted that
\mathcal{W}_{K}
in the statement of
Corollary

3.6
is not equal to the subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
obtained by applying
-\otimes_{k}K
to every object in
\mathcal{W}
, which is usually not a wide subcategory of
\mods\Lambda_{K}
. Indeed, this naïve procedure would not yield a subcategory which is closed under direct summands. This motivates the investigation of individual objects under base field extension rather than of subcategories, as previously explored in
Corollary

2.13
.

3.1.
Left-finite and right-finite semibricks

Let
(P,\leq)
be a poset. Recall that a
cover relation
in
P
is given a pair of elements
p_{1},p_{2}\in P
such that
p_{1}\leq p_{2}
, and if
p_{1}\leq p_{1.5}\leq p_{2}
then
p_{1.5}
is either
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
. We denote this cover relation by
p_{1}\lessdot p_{2}
.
The Hasse quiver
\Hasse(P)
of
P
has as its vertices the elements of
P
and arrows
p_{2}\to p_{1}
for every cover relation
p_{2}\lessdot p_{1}
.
The Hasse quiver of
\tors\Lambda
is particularly well studied, see for example
[
DIR+23
]
. An important feature of
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
is the brick labelling of its arrows. By
[
DIR+23
, Thm. 3.3(b)]
, for every arrow
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
, there exists a unique brick
B
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=\Filt\{B\}
. Thus, we may label the arrow with this brick and denote it by
\mathcal{T}\xrightarrow{B}\mathcal{U}
, see also
[
BCZ19
]
. For the purpose of
\tau
-perpendicular intervals, the following description is particularly important.

Proposition 3.7
.

[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]

[
DIR+23
, Prop. 4.9]

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
correspond to
(M,P)\in\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda
under the bijection of
Theorem

2.10
. Then the cover relations
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}_{i}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
are labelled by distinct isomorphism classes of bricks in the (left-finite) semibrick

(3.a)

\mathcal{S}=\ind(M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M).

Dually, let
(N,Q)=H(M,P)\in\mathrm{s\tau^{-1}\mathchar 45\relax tiltp\,}\Lambda
be the corresponding support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pair. Then the cover relations
\mathcal{S}_{j}\to\mathcal{T}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
are labelled by the distinct isomorphism classes of brick in the (right-finite) semibrick

(3.b)

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda}(N)}N).

Denote the collection of
left-finite
semibricks, that is, semibricks arising as in
Eq.

3.a
for some support
\tau
-tilting pair
(M,P)
, by
\flsbrick\Lambda
and the collection of right-finite semibricks, that is, semibricks arising as in
Eq.

3.b
for some support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pair
(N,Q)
, by
\frsbrick\Lambda
. The following theorem illustrates the importance of the brick labelling for our purpose.

Theorem 3.8
.

[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.16]

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair. Define the torsion classes
\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)}
as in
Section

2.3
.
Then
\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}=\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}
, where
\mathcal{S}
consists of the labels of arrows incident to
\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)}
in
\Hasse[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
. Equivalently,
\mathcal{S}
consists of the labels of arrows incident to
\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}
in
\Hasse[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
.

Therefore, we are especially interested in investigating the left-finite and right-finite semibricks under base field extension.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then the following square is commutative:

(3.c)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\flsbrick\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\flsbrick\Lambda_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{k}K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)}

where the horizontal map at the top is given by
(M,P)\mapsto\ind(M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)
and the horizontal map at the bottom is given by
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})\mapsto\ind(M^{\prime}/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M^{\prime})}M^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
M\in\mathrm{\tau\mathchar 45\relax rigidp}\,\Lambda
. We simply need to show that

\ind(\ind(M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)\simeq\ind(M_{K}/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K})}M_{K}).

Since
-\otimes_{k}K
is additive, we observe that

(3.d)

\ind(\ind(M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)\simeq\ind({(}M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M{)}\otimes_{k}K).

From
Lemma

1.2
we know that
\End_{\Lambda}(M)\otimes_{k}K\simeq\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K})
, and from
Lemma

1.8
(2) we have
(\rad M)_{K}\simeq\rad M_{K}
since
K:k
is MacLane separable. Moreover,
-\otimes_{k}K
is exact, yielding
(M/N)_{K}\simeq M_{K}/N_{K}
. Putting these observations together, we obtain

\displaystyle\ind(\ind(M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)

\displaystyle=\ind((M/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:indindsbrick}})

\displaystyle=\ind(M_{K}/(\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)_{K})

\displaystyle(\text{since $-\otimes_{k}K$ is exact})

\displaystyle=\ind(M_{K}/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)\otimes_{k}K}M_{K})

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Kasradsoclift}(2)})

\displaystyle=\ind(M_{K}/\rad_{\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K})}M_{K})

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Kas00.2.2}}),

as required.
∎

The dual statement follows from a similar proof. It is included for the sake of completeness and for later reference.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then the following square is commutative:

(3.e)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau^{–1}\mathchar 45\relax rigid}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frsbrick\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{s\tau^{–1}\mathchar 45\relax rigid}\Lambda_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frsbrick\Lambda_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{k}K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)}

where the horizontal map at the top is given by
(M,P)\mapsto\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)
and the horizontal map at the bottom is given by
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})\mapsto\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M^{\prime})}M^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
K:k
is MacLane separable, it follows that
(\soc_{\Lambda}M)_{K}\simeq\soc_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K}
by
Lemma

1.8
(3). Similarly to the proof of
Lemma

3.9
, it follows that

\displaystyle\ind(\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)

\displaystyle\simeq\ind((\soc_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)}M)\otimes_{k}K)

(since
-\otimes_{k}K
is additive)

\displaystyle\simeq\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda}(M)\otimes_{k}K}M_{K})

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Kasradsoclift}(3)})

\displaystyle\simeq\ind(\soc_{\End_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K})}M_{K})

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Kas00.2.2}}).

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Let
K:k
be any field extension. Then there exist two injective maps

\mathcal{L}:\flsbrick(\Lambda)\to\flsbrick(\Lambda_{K}),\quad\mathcal{R}:\frsbrick(\Lambda)\to\frsbrick(\Lambda_{K}).

This observation follows from the following two facts:

(1)

The horizontal maps in the commutative diagrams
Eq.

3.c
and
Eq.

3.e
are bijections by
[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]
.

(2)

The scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K
is injective-on-objects and lifts support
\tau
-tilting pairs and support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pairs by
Lemma

2.7
and its dual.

However, unlike in
Lemma

3.9
and
Lemma

3.10
, it is not clear whether
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
are given by
\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)
when
K:k
is not MacLane separable.

4.
The
\tau
-cluster morphism category

The
\tau
-cluster morphism category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
of a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
was sequentially introduced in a series of papers
[
IT17
,
BM21a
,
BH23
]
. It captures the structure of
\tau
-tilting reductions as introduced in
[
Jas15
,
DIR+23
]
. The classifying space of
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is particularly interesting since its fundamental group, known as the picture group
[
ITW16
,
HI21b
]
when
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite, is closely related to maximal green sequences
[
IT21
,
Bø
]
. In certain cases, these classifying spaces are actually
K(\pi,1)
spaces for the picture group
[
BH22
,
HI21b
,
HI21a
,
IT17
,
IT22
,
Kai25
,
Kai24
]
. Furthermore, generalisations of the
\tau
-cluster morphisms and different approaches to it have provided important insights to its structure, see
[
Bør21
,
STTW23
,
Kai25
,
Bør24
,
Kai24
]
. The main result of this section provides a close connection between the
\tau
-cluster morphism category of
\Lambda
and that of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category of
\Lambda_{K}
, where
K:k
is a MacLane separable field extension.

4.1.
Background on the
\tau
-cluster morphism category

We begin by introducing some notation. Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair and recall that the interval
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]\coloneqq[\Fac M,{}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}]\subseteq\tors{\Lambda}
is called
\tau
-perpendicular
. One defines the corresponding
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory
by

(4.a)

\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=M^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}\subseteq\mods{\Lambda}.

The importance of these subcategories comes from the following results.

Theorem 4.1
.

[
Jas15
, Thm. 3.8]

[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.12]
[
BST19
, Thm. 3.14]

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
.

(1)

The
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
is a wide subcategory of
\mods\Lambda
, which is to say that it is closed under kernels, cokernels and extensions.

(2)

The category
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
is equivalent to the module category
\mods{\Gamma}_{(M,P)}
, for a finite-dimensional algebra
{\Gamma}_{(M,P)}
with
|{\Lambda}|-|M|-|P|
isomorphism classes of simple modules.

(3)

There exists an isomorphism of complete lattices

-\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}:[\Fac M,{}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}]\to\tors\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}

which restricts to an order-preserving bijection of functorially finite torsion classes.

As a direct consequence of
Theorem

4.1
(3), we obtain that distinct
\tau
-perpendicular intervals of
\tors A
whose corresponding
\tau
-perpendicular subcategories coincide are related by a lattice isomorphism. In fact, this result holds in much greater generality. Let
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
be an interval in
\tors\Lambda
. By
[
Tat21
, Thm. 3.2]
, the so-called
heart

\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}
of
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
is a quasi-abelian category. Such quasi-abelian categories admit torsion classes, see
[
Tat21
, Def. 2.4]
, which are controller by those of
\tors\Lambda
in our setting.

Theorem 4.2
.

[
Tat21
, Thm. A]

Let
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
be intervals in
\tors\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{2}
. Then there are three lattice isomorphisms, as shown in the diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}{]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}{]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tors(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1})=\tors(\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\perp}}

The inverses of the downward maps are given by

\displaystyle\tors(\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{i})

\displaystyle\to[\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{i}*\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{X\in\mods\Lambda:\exists 0\to U\to X\to V\to 0,U\in\mathcal{U}_{i},V\in\mathcal{V}\}.

The horizontal map in
[
Tat21
, Thm. A]
factors through the downward ones. It is important to understand how the brick labellings of the intervals relate.

Proposition 4.3
.

The horizontal lattice isomorphism between
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
in
Theorem

4.2
preserves the brick labelling of Hasse quivers. Moreover, if
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{i}
is a wide subcategory, then the downward facing maps
-\cap\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\perp}
also preserve the brick labelling.

Proof.

The first statement follows immediately from
[
Tat21
, Lem. 4.7]
. The second statement is
[
AP22
, Thm. 4.2(2)]
.
∎

We now present the definition of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category from the lattice of torsion classes.

Definition 4.4
.

[
Kai24
, Def. 3.3]

The
\tau
-cluster morphism category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
has as its objects equivalence classes
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim}
of
\tau
-perpendicular intervals of
\tors\Lambda
under the equivalence relation

[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{1},P_{1})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{1},P_{1})}]\sim[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{2},P_{2})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{2},P_{2})}]

whenever
\mathcal{W}_{(M_{1},P_{1})}=\mathcal{W}_{(M_{2},P_{2})}
. The morphisms of
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
are given by equivalence classes of morphisms in the poset category
\tauint(\tors\Lambda)
. More precisely,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)}([\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim},[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]_{\sim})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]\in[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim}\\
[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]\in[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]_{\sim}\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\tauint(\tors\Lambda)}([\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}],[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}])

under the equivalence relation

f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{1},P_{1})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{1},P_{1})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N_{1},Q_{1})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{1},Q_{1})}]}\sim f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{2},P_{2})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{2},P_{2})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N_{2},Q_{2})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{2},Q_{2})}]}

whenever
[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{1},Q_{1})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{1},Q_{1})}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M_{1},P_{1})}=[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{2},Q_{2})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{2},Q_{2})}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M_{2},P_{2})}
.

Instead of recalling the definition of composition in
\mathfrak{W}(A)
, we recall the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

[
Kai24
, Lem. 3.6]

Let
\rho_{1}:\mathcal{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{2}
and
\rho_{2}:\mathcal{W}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{3}
be composable morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
. Then there exist
\tau
-perpendicular intervals
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
such that
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
is a representative of
\rho_{1}
,
[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
is a representative of
\rho_{2}
, and
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
is a representative of
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
.

This category may equivalently be defined using
\tau
-perpendicular subcategories and
\tau
-rigid pairs
[
BM21a
,
BH23
]
, 2-term silting objects
[
Bør21
]
or the
g
-vector fan
[
STTW23
]
. In its different guises, various aspects of this category have been investigated. The starting point for all endeavours regarding this category is the fact that the classifying space
\mathcal{B}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is a cube complex whose fundamental group is the picture group
G(\Lambda)
of
[
ITW16
,
HI21b
]
, see
[
HI21b
,
IT22
,
Kai25
]
. The picture group is an important object with close connections to, for example, maximal green sequences
[
IT21
,
Bø
]
. As such, if
\mathcal{B}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is a
K(\pi,1)
space the relationship between the
\tau
-cluster morphism category and the picture group is particularly intimate. The main tool for determining whether
\mathcal{B}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is a
K(\pi,1)
space are the sufficient conditions developed in
[
Gro87
]
for cube complexes to be (locally) CAT(0), which have been translated into a categorical setting in
[
Igu14
]
.

Proposition 4.6
.

[
Igu14
, Prop 3.5, 3.8]

If the following three conditions are satisfied, then
\mathcal{B}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
is locally CAT(0). In particular, it is then a
K(\pi,1)
space:

(1)

There is a faithful functor
\Psi:\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to G
for some group
G
considered as a groupoid with one object;

(2)

The category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
satisfies the pairwise-compatibility condition of first factors;

(3)

The category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
satisfies the pairwise-compatibility condition of last factors.

If Condition (1) holds, we say that
\Lambda

admits a faithful group functor
.
The focus of the remaining section lies on the existence of faithful group functors, which has been conjectured to hold for all finite-dimensional
k
-algebras
[
HI21a
]
1
1

1

The cited reference assumes
\tau
-tilting finiteness when making this conjecture, but this seems unnecessary in hindsight. At the time
[
HI21a
]
written, the definition of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category was only available for
\tau
-tilting finite
k
-algebras
[
BM21a
]
. It has since been extended to all finite-dimensional
k
-algebras
[
BH23
,
Bør21
]
. We refer to the Appendix, where this conjecture has been restated in
A.11
. Various examples of algebras satisfying Condition (1) are known
[
HI21b
,
HI21a
,
IT22
,
Kai25
,
Kai24
,
Bø
]
. In particular, if
k
is algebraically closed and of characteristic 0, this question is addressed in
[
Bø
]
using Ringel–Hall algebras of locally constructible functions. Condition (2) of
Proposition

4.6
is always satisfied due to the structure of
\tau
-rigid pairs, whereas some examples of algebras are known not to satisfy Condition (3), see
[
BH22
,
HI21a
,
IT22
]
. Before considering Condition (1) in more detail, we need to gather more preliminary results.

4.2.
Further results about field extensions

In this section we collect some further results about the objects involved in
Definition

4.4
under base field extension. The first one is a simple observation.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
K:k
be a field extension, let
(M,P)
be a support
\tau
-tilting pair in
\mods\Lambda
and let
N\in\mods\Lambda
. Then,
N\in\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
if and only if
N_{K}\in\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
.

Proof.

The notation
\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}\subseteq\mods\Lambda_{K}
is well-defined since
(M_{K},P_{K})
is a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
by
Lemma

2.4
. Then, using
Lemma

1.2
the following hold:

(1)

\Hom_{\Lambda}(M,N)=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(M_{K},N_{K})=0
;

(2)

\Hom_{\Lambda}(P,N)=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K},N_{K})=0
;

(3)

\Hom_{\Lambda}(N,\tau_{\Lambda}M)=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(N_{K},(\tau_{\Lambda}M)_{K})=0
if and only if
\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(N_{K},\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}M_{K})=0
by
Lemma

2.2
.

Therefore the result follows from the definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
and
\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
.
∎

The study of left and right-finite semibricks in
Section

3.1
is motivated by the powerful description of such semibricks as labels of
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
in
Proposition

3.7
. This allows us to describe the relative simple modules of the
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory in terms of left-finite and right-finite semibricks. Our next result is has ties to
[
AP22
,
BH23
]
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Write
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}
for the left-finite semibrick corresponding to
(M^{+},P)
as in
Eq.

3.a
, see
Proposition

3.7
, and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
for the right-finite semibrick corresponding to the support
\tau^{-1}
-tilting pair
H(M^{-},P^{-})
as in
Eq.

3.b
, with
H
as in
Eq.

2.b
. Then the
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
as defined in
Eq.

4.a
is given by

\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}\},

Proof.

Write
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]=[\Fac M,{}^{\perp}\tau M\cap P^{\perp}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda
for the corresponding
\tau
-perpendicular interval. By
Theorem

3.5
, it is possible to write
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}
for some semibrick
\mathcal{S}\in\sbrick\Lambda
and by
[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.16(a)(c)]
,
\mathcal{S}
consists of labels of arrows incident to
\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
. However, by
Proposition

3.7
, the right-finite semibrick
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
consists of labels of all arrows going into
\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
, therefore
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
. Dually by
[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.16(a)(d)]
,
\mathcal{S}
consists of labels of arrows incident to
\mathcal{T}
in
\Hasse[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
. By
Proposition

3.7
again, the left-finite semibrick
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}
consists of labels of all arrows going out of
\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
. It follows that
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
.

Conversely, assume that there is a brick
S\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
. By
Proposition

3.7
,
S
labels an arrow
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
and an arrow
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
in
\Hasse(\tors\Lambda)
. However, by definition of the brick labelling this means that
S\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
and
S\in\mathcal{T}
, so that
S\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
. In particular, if
\mathrm{T}(S)
denotes the smallest torsion class of containing
S
, we have that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}\lor\mathrm{T}(S)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, so
S
is labels an arrow adjacent to
\mathcal{U}
in
\Hasse[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
, and thus
S\in\mathcal{S}
by
[
DIR+23
, Thm. 4.16]
.
∎

In particular, it allows us to apply the result of
Section

3.1
to study wide subcategories under base field extension.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension, and
(M,P)
be a
\tau_{\Lambda}
-rigid pair whose
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory is given by
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}
for some semibrick
\mathcal{S}
. Then

\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}=\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}(\mathcal{S}_{K}).

Proof.

Using
Lemma

4.8
, write
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)}
. By
Lemma

1.1
(6), the equality
\mathcal{S}_{K}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)})_{K}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)})_{K}\cap(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)})_{K}
holds. Combining
Lemma

2.23
and
Lemma

3.9
gives the equality

\ind((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M,P)})_{K})=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M_{K},P_{K})}\in\flsbrick\Lambda_{K},

and similarly combining
Lemma

2.22
,
Lemma

2.16
and
Lemma

3.10
gives the equality

\ind((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(M,P)})_{K})=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(M_{K},P_{K})}\in\frsbrick\Lambda_{K}.

Since
\Filt_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{S}_{K})=\Filt_{\Lambda}(\add\mathcal{S}_{K})
by definition and
\add\mathcal{S}_{K}
is closed under direct summands, the result follows from
Lemma

4.8
.
∎

It is not difficult to see that
Proposition

4.9
concerns objects of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category. To investigate the morphisms of
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
the following equivalent condition for the identification of morphisms in
Definition

4.4
is necessary.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(M,P)
and
(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})
be
\tau
-rigid pairs in
\mods\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}
. Moreover, let
(N,Q)
and
(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
be
\tau
-rigid pairs in
\mods\Lambda
such that
M\in\add(N)
,
P\in\add(Q)
,
M^{\prime}\in\add(N^{\prime})
and
P^{\prime}\in\add(Q^{\prime})
. Assume also that
\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]\cap\mathcal{W}=[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]\cap\mathcal{W}
;

(2)

\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
;

(3)

\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
;

(4)

\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}

(5)

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{R}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
;

(6)

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

The implications
(1)\iff(2)
,
(2)\implies(3)
,
(2)\implies(4)
and
(3)+(4)\implies(2)
are immediate.

(3)\implies(4)
Since
\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}
, we compute

\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=(\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W})*\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=(\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime}))}\cap\mathcal{W})*\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}=\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}.

Here the first and last equalities follow from a standard argument.

(4)\implies(3)
Since
\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}
, we compute

\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=(\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W})\cap{}^{\perp}\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=(\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W})\cap{}^{\perp}\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}=\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}.

Here the first and last equalities follow directly from the dual of
[
ES22
, Lem. 2.8]
.

(4)\implies(6)
. Let
\mathcal{X}\in\ftors\mathcal{W}
, then there exists a unique (relative) left-finite semibrick
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{X}}\in\flsbrick\mathcal{W}
corresponding to it by
[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]
and
Theorem

2.10
. By
Proposition

3.7
, it labels the arrows going out of
\mathcal{X}
in
\Hasse(\tors\mathcal{W})
, and by
Theorem

4.1
, there exists a unique
\mathcal{Y}\in[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda
such that now
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}\cap\mathcal{W}
. It follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{Y}}\cap\mathcal{W}
, since the intersection with
\mathcal{W}
preserves the brick labels by
Proposition

4.3
. Consequently, since
\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}\in\tors\mathcal{W}
, it follows that

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}.

(3)\implies(5)
follows from an entirely analogous argument.

(6)\implies(4)
. Using the same notation and reasoning as in
(4)\implies(6)
, the assumption implies

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}}.

Then it follows directly from
[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]
that
\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}
, as required.

(5)\implies(2)
follows from an entirely analogous argument.
∎

Notice that
Lemma

4.10
(1) states the defintion of when morphisms
f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]}
and
f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]}
from the
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}
to the
\tau
-perpendicular subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}
are identified in
\mathfrak{W}(A)
. Thus, the equivalent conditions give various simpler ways of identifying morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(A)
.

The following lemma, in combination with the previous, is important to control the identification of morphisms when studying the
\tau
-cluster morphism category under scalar extension.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
(M,P)
be a
\tau
-rigid pair in
\mods\Lambda
. Then the following square is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\in\flsbrick\Lambda\\
\text{s.t. }(N,Q)\in{[}(M^{-},P^{-}),(M^{+},P){]}\subseteq\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\end{subarray}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\flsbrick\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\in\flsbrick\Lambda_{K}\\
\text{s.t. }(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in{[}(M_{K}^{-},P_{K}^{-}),(M_{K}^{+},P_{K}){]}\subseteq\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda_{K}\end{subarray}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\flsbrick\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ind(-\otimes_{k}K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}}

and the horizontal maps are bijections.

Proof.

The vertical maps are well-defined by
Proposition

2.24
and
Lemma

3.9
. Moreover, the horizontal map given by intersection with
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
defines a brick label preserving isomorphism from
\Hasse[(M^{-},P^{-}),(M^{+},P)]
to
\Hasse(\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)})
by
Proposition

4.3
. As discussed in the proof of
Lemma

4.10
, the horizontal map is therefore well-defined, since the brick labelling is preserved and left-finite semibricks can be read of as the labels of arrows going out of a support
\tau
-tilting pair in the Hasse quiver by
Proposition

3.7
. The same holds for the bottom horizontal map. The horizontal maps are bijections because the intervals of support
\tau
-tilting pairs are in bijection by
Theorem

4.1
and support
\tau
-tilting pairs are in bijection with left-finite semibricks by
[
Asa20
, Thm. 1.3]
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}
. By
Lemma

4.7
,
S\in\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}
if and only if
S_{K}\in\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
. In particular, every indecomposable direct summand
S^{\prime}\in\add(S_{K})
is contained in
\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
. Therefore, the square indeed commutes.
∎

We have collected all the preliminary results to establish the main theorem of this section.

4.3.
Faithful functors under base field extension

As described earlier, the existence of a faithful functor from
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
to a groupoid with one object has important consequences and is a sufficient condition for the classifying space to be a
K(\pi,1)
space. The main result relating to the
\tau
-cluster morphism category is the following result extending the existence of faithful group functors algebras over reasonably nice subfields. All preliminary results have been collected in the previous subsection.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension. There exists a well-defined faithful functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
given by

(4.b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})

\displaystyle[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim}

\displaystyle\mapsto[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]_{\sim}

\displaystyle[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}][\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]}]

\displaystyle\mapsto[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]}].

Consequently, if
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
admits a faithful group functor, so does
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathcal{F}
is well-defined on objects.
It is shown in
Proposition

2.24
that if
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
is a
\tau_{\Lambda}
-perpendicular interval in
\tors\Lambda
, then
[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]
is a
\tau_{\Lambda_{K}}
-perpendicular interval in
\tors\Lambda_{K}
. Consider two
\tau_{\Lambda}
-perpendicular intervals
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]
in
\tors\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}=\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}
for some
\mathcal{S}\in\sbrick\Lambda
. It follows from
Proposition

4.9
that

\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}=\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{S_{K}\}=\mathcal{W}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}.

Therefore, if
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim}=[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]_{\sim}
in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
, then

[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]_{\sim}=[\mathcal{U}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}]_{\sim}

in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
. This shows that
\mathcal{F}
is well-defined on objects.

To investigate the morphisms, consider two
\tau_{\Lambda}
-perpendicular intervals
[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]
such that
[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda
. Then the inclusion

[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda_{K},

follows from the fact that
-\otimes_{k}K:\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\to\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda_{K}
-preserves the partial order, as shown in
Lemma

2.11
.
Now, consider four
\tau_{\Lambda}
-perpendicular intervals:

[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]\text{ and }[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]

in
\tors\Lambda
such that
\Filt_{A}\{\mathcal{S}\}=\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}
for some
\mathcal{S}\in\sbrick\Lambda
,
\mathcal{W}_{(N,Q)}=\mathcal{W}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}
and moreover

[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}\subseteq\tors\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}.

Then there is a chain of implications

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M,P)}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}=\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\}

(by
Lemma

4.10
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ind((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N,Q)}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\})_{K})=\ind((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda}\{\mathcal{S}\})_{K})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{(N_{K},Q_{K})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{\mathcal{S}_{K}\}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{L}}^{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{\mathcal{S}_{K}\}

(by
Lemma

4.11
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{\mathcal{S}_{K}\}=\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}\cap\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{\mathcal{S}_{K}\}

(by
Lemma

4.10
)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}=\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}\cap\mathcal{W}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}

(by
Proposition

4.9
)

Therefore,
Lemma

4.10
yields that

[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}=[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}]\cap\mathcal{W}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}

in
\tors\Filt_{\Lambda_{K}}\{\mathcal{S}_{K}\}
. In other words, identification of morphisms is preserved, whence
\mathcal{F}
determines a well-defined map on the morphism sets. To investigate the composition of morphisms, let

[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})}],\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})}]}\circ[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]}]

be two composable morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
. Then from
Lemma

4.5
we know that there exists an interval
[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})}]\subseteq[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]
such that

[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})}]}]=[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})}]}]

so that their composition is given by

[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})}]}].

It is immediate from the definitions that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}([f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})}]}])\circ\mathcal{F}([f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime},P^{\prime})}]}])

\displaystyle=[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{((M^{\prime\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((M^{\prime\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime\prime})_{K})}]}]

\displaystyle\qquad\circ[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((M^{\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime})_{K})}]}]

\displaystyle=[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{((M^{\prime\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((M^{\prime\prime})_{K},(P^{\prime\prime})_{K})}]}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}([f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(M^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime\prime})}]}]).

Thus
\mathcal{F}
preserves composition of morphisms. It is clear that
\mathcal{F}
preserves identity morphisms. Therefore
\mathcal{F}:\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
is a well-defined functor.

Finally, to see that
\mathcal{F}
is faithful, take two distinct morphisms

(4.c)

\displaystyle[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]}],

\displaystyle[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}][\mathcal{U}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})},\mathcal{T}_{(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})}]}]

\displaystyle\in\Hom_{\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)}([\mathcal{U}_{(M,P)},\mathcal{T}_{(M,P)}]_{\sim},[\mathcal{U}_{(N,Q)},\mathcal{T}_{(N,Q)}]_{\sim}).

which may be taken to be represented two morphisms in
\tauint(\tors A)
with the same domain by aspecial case of
Lemma

4.5
lemma, see also
[
Kai25
, Cor. 3.6]
. Assume that

[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}]}]=[f_{[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}][\mathcal{U}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}]}],

in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
, which is to lead to a contradiction. This would mean that the intervals

[\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}]\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathcal{U}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}]

coincide as a result of
Theorem

4.2
. Indeed, their intersections with
\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
coincide by assumption and the lattice
\tors\mathcal{W}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}
is isomorphic to the interval
[\mathcal{U}_{(M_{K},P_{K})},\mathcal{T}_{(M_{K},P_{K})}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda_{K}
by
Theorem

4.2
. Using
Lemma

2.22
and
Lemma

2.23
, this implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}=\mathcal{U}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}_{(N_{K},Q_{K})}=\mathcal{T}_{((N^{\prime})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow((N_{K})^{-},(Q_{K})^{-})=((((N^{\prime})_{K})^{-},(Q^{\prime})_{K})^{-})\quad\text{and}\quad((N_{K})^{+},Q_{K})=((N^{\prime})_{K}^{+},(Q^{\prime})_{K})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow((N^{-})_{K},(Q^{-})_{K})=(((N^{\prime})^{-})_{K},((Q^{\prime})^{-})_{K})\quad\text{and}\quad((N^{+})_{K},Q_{K})=(((N^{\prime})^{+})_{K},(Q^{\prime})_{K}).

Since
-\otimes_{k}K
defines an injective map of support
\tau
-tilting pairs by
Proposition

2.12
, it follows that
(N^{-},Q^{-})=((N^{\prime})^{-},(Q^{\prime})^{-})
and
(N^{+},Q)=((N^{\prime})^{+},Q^{\prime})
. Since two distinct
\tau
-rigid pairs cannot both have the same Bongartz completion and the same co-Bongartz completion, this yields
(N,Q)=(N^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. However, this is a contradicts the assumption that the morphisms chosen in (
4.c
) are distinct. We conclude that the functor
\mathcal{F}
is faithful.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic 0 and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra. Then
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor.

Proof.

Let
\overline{k}
denote an algebraic closure of
k
. Since
k
is of characteristic 0, the field extension
\overline{k}:k
is MacLane separable by definition. The
\overline{k}
-algebra
\Lambda_{\overline{k}}
is a finite-dimensional algebra over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. It is shown in
[
Bø
]
that
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\overline{k}})
admits a faithful group functor. Thus, we have that
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor, obtained by composition with the faithful functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
in
Theorem

4.12
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

Suppose that
k
is algebraically closed. In
[
Non25b
]
, the author considers the tensor product of a hereditary finite-dimensional
k
-algebra
\Lambda
with a finite-dimensional local commutative
k
-algebra
R
. It is shown that the functor
-\otimes_{k}R:\mods\Lambda\to\mods\Lambda\otimes_{k}R
commutes with the Auslander–Reiten translation and the Nakayama functor
[
Non25b
, Prop. 1.8]
. By
Lemma

2.1
and
Lemma

2.2
, the scalar extension functor
-\otimes_{k}K
of a field extension shares these properties. In
[
Non25b
]
it is shown that this leads to a bijection of
\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\leftrightarrow\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,(\Lambda\otimes_{k}R)

[
Non25b
, Cor. 1.9]
and an equivalence of categories
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\simeq\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda\otimes_{k}R)

[
Non25b
, Thm. 6.17]
. Given a field extension
K:k
, there is an injection
\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda\hookrightarrow\mathrm{s\tau\mathchar 45\relax tiltp}\,\Lambda_{K}
by
Lemma

2.7
and, if
K:k
is MacLane separable, a faithful functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{K})
by
Theorem

4.12
. It would be interesting to investigate if a simultaneous generalisation is possible. In this spirit, we refer to
[
IK24
]
and
[
LZ23
]
, where some questions regarding silting theory have been studied in a general setting.

4.4.
Worked examples

As an interlude, we spend a subsection spelling out our results so far for a few simple examples. It is necessary to recall the notion of species, a generalisation of quivers.

Definition 4.15
.

[
Gab73
]

Let
k
be any field. A
k
-species

\mathbb{S}=(Q^{\mathbb{S}},\{D_{a}\}_{a\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{0}},\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{1}})
consists of the data of:

•

A quiver
Q^{\mathbb{S}}=(Q_{0}^{\mathbb{S}},Q_{1}^{\mathbb{S}})
;

•

A division
k
-algebra
D_{a}
for each
a\in Q_{0}^{\mathbb{S}}
;

•

A
D_{a}\mathchar 45\relax D_{b}
-bimodule
X_{\alpha}
for each
a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b
in
Q_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}
.

We remark that if
k
algebraically closed then every divison
k
-algebra is isomorphic to
k
itself.
More generally, if every division
k
-algebra
D_{a}
is
k
and every
D_{a}\mathchar 45\relax D_{b}
-bimodule (or just
k\mathchar 45\relax k
-bimodule)
X_{\alpha}
is
k
, then the
k
-species encodes the same information as a quiver.

Given a
k
-species
\mathbb{S}=(Q^{\mathbb{S}},\{D_{a}\}_{a\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{0}},\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{1}})
, let
D
denote the semisimple
k
-algebra
\prod_{a\in Q_{0}}D_{a}
and let
X
denote the abelian group
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q_{1}}X_{\alpha}
. Extending the bimodule structure of each
X_{\alpha}
in the standard way, one equips
X
with the structure of
D
-
D
-bimodule. The
tensor path algebra
of
\mathbb{S}
is then defined as the tensor
k
-algebra

k\mathbb{S}\coloneqq T_{D}({X})=\bigoplus_{\ell\geq 0}X^{\otimes_{D}\ell},

where
X^{\otimes_{D}0}=D
, and in which the multiplication is given by the linear extension of the following rule:

(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p})\cdot(y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes y_{q})=x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p}\otimes y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes y_{q},

where the first factor on the left hand side is in
X^{\otimes_{D}p}
and the second is in
X^{\otimes_{D}q}
. If every division
k
-algebra
D_{a}
is
k
and every
k\mathchar 45\relax k
-bimodule
X_{\alpha}
is
k
, the tensor path algebra of the species
\mathbb{S}
becomes the ordinary path
k
-algebra of the underlying quiver
Q^{\mathbb{S}}
.

In the following, most of our examples will be constructed using species over the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
. For an
\mathbb{R}
-species
\mathbb{S}
and an ideal
I
of the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}
, we will consider the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/I
. It will be useful to know how to compute the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
, obtained by scalar extension along
\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}
.
2
2

2

Recall that a field
k
is
real closed
if it is not algebraically closed, and an algebraic closure
\overline{k}
is a finite extension of
k
. By the Artin–Schreier Theorem, the splitting field of the polynomial
x^{2}+1
is then an algebraic closure of
{k}

[
AS27
]
. The assertions in
Proposition

4.16
remain true if we replace
\mathbb{R}
with any other real closed field
k
, and
\mathbb{C}
by
\overline{k}
,
mutatis mutandis
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-species. Suppose that the division
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
D_{a}
is either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
for all vertices
a\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{0}
. Assume also that each bimodule
X_{\alpha}
is simple for each arrow
\alpha\in Q^{\mathbb{S}}_{1}
.

(1)

There is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra isomorphism
\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{C}Q
, where the quiver
Q
is constructed as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\,:\,a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\in Q_{1},\,D_{a}=D_{b}=\mathbb{R}\}

\displaystyle\sqcup

\displaystyle\{a\xrightarrow{\underline{\alpha}}\underline{b},\,a\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}\overline{b}\,:\,a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\in Q_{1},\,D_{a}=\mathbb{R},\,D_{b}=\mathbb{C}\}

\displaystyle\sqcup

\displaystyle\{\underline{a}\xrightarrow{\underline{\alpha}}b,\,\overline{a}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}b\,:\,a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\in Q_{1},\,D_{a}=\mathbb{C},\,D_{b}=\mathbb{R}\}

\displaystyle\sqcup

\displaystyle\{\underline{a}\xrightarrow{\underline{\alpha}}\underline{b},\,\overline{a}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}\overline{b}\,:\,a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\in Q_{1},\,D_{a}=D_{b}=\mathbb{C},X_{\alpha}={}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}}\}

\displaystyle\sqcup

\displaystyle\{\overline{a}\xrightarrow{\underline{\alpha}}\underline{b},\,\underline{a}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}\overline{b}\,:\,a\xrightarrow{\alpha}b\in Q_{1},\,D_{a}=D_{b}=\mathbb{C},\,X_{\alpha}={}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\},

where
{}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the natural
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathbb{C}
-bimodule
\mathbb{C}
, and
{}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathbb{C}
-bimodule
\mathbb{C}
equipped with the bimodule structure given by
x\curvearrowright z\curvearrowleft y=xz{y}^{\ast}
, for
x,y\in\mathbb{C}
, where
{y}^{\ast}
denotes the complex conjugate of
y
. We remark that
{}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
{}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
are the only simple
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathbb{C}
-bimodules up to isomorphism, so that this description covers all cases, given our assumptions that the division
\mathbb{R}
-algebras
D_{a}
can only be
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
and that each bimodule
X_{\alpha}
is simple.

(2)

We have that
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/\langle X\otimes_{D}X\rangle)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}Q/\langle\text{paths of length 2}\rangle
.

Proof.

For (
1
), we refer to
[
Li23
, §3.2]
.
We prove (
2
) through the following steps

(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/\langle X\otimes_{D}X\rangle)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\simeq{(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S})_{\mathbb{C}}\over{\langle{X_{\mathbb{C}}}\otimes_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}X_{\mathbb{C}}\rangle}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}Q\over\langle\text{paths of length 2}\rangle},

where the first step uses the fact that
(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/\langle X\rangle)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong D_{\mathbb{C}}
is semisimple by
Theorem

1.7
(
1
), and the last step follows since
\langle X\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}Q}=\langle\text{paths of length 1}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}Q}\subseteq\mathbb{C}Q
by (
1
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Using
Proposition

4.16
, we give two worked examples.

; we write
\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket
, we mean
{[\Fac Y,\Fac X]_{\sim}}
. Note that, among other identifications, the corners of the outer hexagonal shape are identified to be the same object in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
. If
[\Fac Y^{\prime},\Fac X^{\prime}]
is a subinterval of
[\Fac Y,\Fac X]
in
\ftors\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
, there is a morphism
\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
Y^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket
in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
. With the exception of the
orange
morphisms, we only display the
irreducible morphisms

\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
, i.e. they cannot be factored nontrivially. The
orange
morphisms are images of irreducible morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
under the faithful functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\to\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
, see
Theorem

4.12
. Morphisms with the same label (
a
,
b
,
c
or
d
) are to be identified.

Figure 2
.
The diagram displays a subcategory of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
, where the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\Lambda
is as in
Example

4.18
.
Alike to
Figure

1
, we save space by
not
employing the same notation as in
Definition

4.4
; we write
\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket
, we mean
{[\Fac Y,\Fac X]_{\sim}}
. If
[\Fac Y^{\prime},\Fac X^{\prime}]
is a subinterval of
[\Fac Y,\Fac X]
in
\ftors\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
, there is a morphism
\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X\\
Y\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\!\begin{smallmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
Y^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\!\right\rrbracket
in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
. Some of the morphisms have been labelled, and morphisms with the same label are identified. In particular, the upper “hexagon” is to be identified with the lower “hexagon” (and under further identifications, these classifying spaces of these “hexagons” are seen to become 2-tori). The
orange
subdiagram is precisely the (essential) image of the faithful functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{}\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
, see
Theorem

4.12
. Morphisms labelled by
blue
fractured letters, namely
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathfrak{p}}
–
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathfrak{s}}
, are images of irreducible morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
, yet they are not irreducible in
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
.

5.
\tau
-tilting finiteness and
\mathbf{g}
-tameness under base field extension

Drozd’s celebrated trichotomy groups all finite-dimensional algebras over algebraically closed fields into one of three types: finite, tame and wild. A generalisation to hereditary finite-dimensional algebras over arbitrary fields can be made; one determines whether the associated symmetric bilinear form on the Grothendieck group is positive definite, positive semidefinite or indefinite.
We remind the reader that representation finiteness of an algebra is preserved by MacLane separable field extensions, see
Theorem

1.7
(
2
).

Theorem
1.7
.

[
JL82
, Thm. 3.3]

If
K:k
is MacLane separable, then
\Lambda
is representation finite if and only if
\Lambda_{K}
is representation finite. In this case, every indecomposable
\Lambda_{K}
-module is a direct summand of a
\Lambda_{K}
-module
M_{K}
for some indecomposable
M\in\mods\Lambda
.

The class of
\tau
-tilting finite algebras is strictly larger than that of representation finite ones. Since
\tau
-rigid modules are preserved under base field extension by
Lemma

2.4
, it is a natural question to ask whether
\tau
-tilting finiteness is preserved under base field extensions.

In contrast, tame representation type is only a well-defined notion over algebraically closed base fields. Over an arbitrary base field, one can regard generic tameness as a generalisation, since the definitions are equivalent in the algebraically closed case
[
CB91
, Thm. 4.4]
. Kasjan shows that generic tameness is preserved under separable algebraic scalar extension
[
Kas01
, Thm. 4.3]
.

One possible “
\tau
-tilted” version of tameness is
g
-tameness, a notion we recall presently. Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra admitting
n
isomorphism classes of simple modules.
We then say that
\Lambda
is
g
-tame
if the union of the cones in its
g
-vector fan is dense in the ambient Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}

[
AY23
]
. If
k
is algebraically closed, then any tame
k
-algebra is
g
-tame
[
PY23
]
. For other generalisations of the notion of tameness using
\tau
-tilting theory see
[
Pfe25
]
and the references therein.

Example 5.1
.

Consider the
\mathbb{R}
-species
\mathbb{S}
with underlying quiver

with the division
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}
associated to the vertex
1
and the
\mathbb{C}
-
\mathbb{C}
-bimodule
{}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
associated to each loop
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
.
To summarise, let
\mathbb{S}=(Q,\{D_{1}=\mathbb{C}\},\{X_{\alpha}={}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}},X_{\beta}={}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}},X_{\gamma}={}_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\})
. Referring to the discussion following
Definition

4.15
, we may define the tensor path algebra
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}
. Now, consider the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra

\Lambda\simeq\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/\langle X\otimes_{D}X\rangle.
It is clear from the explicit description that the only nonzero idempotent of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{S}/\langle X\otimes_{D}X\rangle
corresponds to the simple
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
D=\mathbb{C}
. Thus,
\Lambda
is a local
\mathbb{R}
-algebra, so in particular, it is
\tau
-tilting finite. Since the cones of the
g
-vector fan of a
\tau
-tilting finite
k
-algebra cover the ambient Euclidean space
[
Asa21
, Thm. 4.7]
, we have that
\Lambda
is
g
-tame.
Following the description given in
Proposition

4.16
, we find that
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to the path
\mathbb{C}
-algebra of the quiver

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\gamma}}

modulo the square of the arrow ideal. The algebra
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
may be obtained by gluing together, in the sense of
[
AHI+23
, Thm. 4.2]
, two 3-Kronecker algebras

\mathbb{C}^{3}&\mathbb{C}\end{pmatrix}.

Each algebra
\Lambda_{\underline{1}}
and
\Lambda_{\overline{1}}
are well-known to not be
g
-tame, and the description of the
g
-vector fan of algebra
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
obtained by gluing immediately yields that
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
is not
g
-tame. Thus, we have produced a counter-example to both the assertion that
\tau
-tilting finiteness is preserved by scalar extension along
\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}
as well as the assertation that
g
-tameness is preserved by scalar extension along
\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{R}
.

5.1.
Cases where
\tau
-tilting finiteness is preserved

Nonetheless, it seems that a generalisation of
Theorem
does hold in many cases. We will not give precise conditions for this on this occasion, but rather use the following family of algebras as an illustrating example.

Definition 5.2
.

[
BGL87
]

Let
k
be any field, and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional hereditary
k
-algebra.
3
3

3

In
[
BGL87
]
, it is also assumed that
\Lambda
is a basic
k
-algebra. However, this is not a necessary assumption in the following
[
CB99
, Thm. 2.3, Cor. 5.5]
.
Consider a projective
\Lambda
-module
P
. The
preprojective algebra
for
P
is defined as the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
k
-algebra

\Pi^{{\Lambda}}(P)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Pi_{i}^{{\Lambda}}(P),

where
\Pi_{i}^{{\Lambda}}(P)=\Hom_{\Lambda}(P,(\tau_{\Lambda}^{-1})^{i}P)
and multiplication
\Pi_{r}^{{\Lambda}}(P)\times\Pi_{s}^{{\Lambda}}(P)\to\Pi_{s+r}^{{\Lambda}}(P)
is given by

(5.a)

u_{r}u_{s}=((\tau_{\Lambda}^{-1})^{r}(u_{r}))\circ u_{s},\quad u_{r}\in\Pi_{r}^{{\Lambda}}(P),u_{s}\in\Pi_{s}^{{\Lambda}}(P).

For
P=\Lambda
, the
k
-algebra
\Pi(P)
is called the
preprojective algebra
of
\Lambda
.

Other definitions of preprojective algebras occur in different settings. The most common definitions are shown to be equivalent in
[
BGL87
, Prop. 3.1]
and
[
Rin98
]
. As a result, the construction giving rise to preprojective algebras will now be shown to commute with taking MacLane separable base field extensions.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
K:k
be a MacLane separable field extension and let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional hereditary
k
-algebra. For every projective module
P\in\proj\Lambda
, there is an isomorphism
(\Pi^{\Lambda}(P))_{K}\simeq\Pi^{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K})
of
K
-algebras.
In other words, taking preprojective algebras commutes with field extension.

Proof.

By
Theorem

1.7
(
1
), we have that
\Lambda_{K}
is a hereditary
K
-algebra. We use
Lemma

1.1
(4) to argue that
P_{K}
is a projective
\Lambda
-module.
For
r\geq 0
, consider the
k
-linear map
\varphi_{r}\colon\Pi^{\Lambda}_{r}(P)\to\Pi^{\Lambda_{K}}_{r}(P_{K})
sending
P\xrightarrow{u_{r}}(\tau^{-1}_{\Lambda})^{r}P
to
P_{K}\xrightarrow{u_{r}\otimes 1}(\tau^{-1}_{\Lambda_{K}})^{r}P_{K}
. Let
\varphi
denote the linear extension
\Pi^{\Lambda}(P)\to\Pi^{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K})
.
Since we can decompose
\varphi\otimes_{k}K
into the following sequence of isomorphisms, we have that
\varphi\otimes_{k}K
is an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces.

\displaystyle\Pi^{\Lambda}(P)\otimes_{k}K

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Pi_{i}^{\Lambda}(P)\right)\otimes_{k}K

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\left(\Pi_{i}^{\Lambda}(P)\otimes_{k}K\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Hom_{\Lambda}(P,(\tau_{\Lambda}^{-1})^{i}P)\otimes_{k}K

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K},((\tau_{\Lambda}^{-1})^{i}P)_{K})

(by
Lemma

1.2
)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Hom_{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K},(\tau^{{-1}}_{\Lambda_{K}})^{i}P_{K})

(by the dual of
Lemma

2.2
)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Pi_{i}^{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K})

\displaystyle\simeq\Pi^{\Lambda_{K}}(P_{K})

It remains to check that
\varphi
is compatible with multiplication. It suffices to show that
\varphi(u_{r}u_{s})=\varphi_{r}(u_{r})\varphi_{s}(u_{s})
for all
r,s\geq 0
. We find that

\displaystyle\varphi(u_{r}u_{s})

\displaystyle=u_{r}u_{s}\otimes 1

\displaystyle=\big(((\tau_{{\Lambda}}^{-1})^{r}(u_{r}))\circ u_{s}\big)\otimes 1

(by
Eq.

5.a
)

\displaystyle=((\tau_{{\Lambda}}^{-1})^{r}(u_{r})\otimes 1)\circ(u_{s}\otimes 1)

(functoriality of
-\otimes_{k}K
)

\displaystyle=((\tau_{{\Lambda_{{K}}}}^{-1})^{r}(u_{r}{\otimes 1}))\circ(u_{s}\otimes 1)

(by the dual of
Lemma

2.2
)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{r}(u_{r})\varphi_{s}(u_{s}),

which is what we wanted to show, concluding the proof.
∎

This observation is particularly interesting because preprojective algebras of representation finite hereditary algebras with sufficiently many isomorphism classes of simple modules are representation infinite. Such examples provide nontrivial (meaning representation infinite) examples such that
\tau
-tilting finiteness is preserved by
[
AHI+22
, Thm. 7.9]
, see also
Theorem

0.8
.
