Now, the preprojective algebra
\Pi(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}})
of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
is well-known to be representation infinite but
\tau
-tilting finite. As a consequence of
Theorem

1.7
(
2
) and
Proposition

5.3
, the algebra
\Pi(\Lambda)
must therefore be representation infinite. Moreover, by
[
AHI+22
, Thm. 7.9]
, the algebra
\Pi(\Lambda)
is
\tau
-tilting finite. Therefore,
\Pi(\Lambda)
is an example of a representation infinite
\tau
-tilting finite algebra which remains
\tau
-tilting finite under base field extension.

We conclude the study of
\tau
-tilting finiteness under base field extension with a general result.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
K:k
be a separable algebraic field extension and let
\Lambda
be a
\tau
-tilting finite
k
-algebra. Let
M\in\mods\Lambda
and consider an indecomposable direct summand
N\in\add(M_{K})
. If
N\in\brick\Lambda_{K}
implies
M\in\brick\Lambda
, then
\Lambda_{K}
is also
\tau
-tilting finite.

Proof.

By the assumption on
K:k
and
[
Kas00
, Prop. 4.13]
, every indecomposable
\Lambda_{K}
-module arises a direct summand of
M_{K}
for some
M\in\mods\Lambda
, so in particular, every brick
B\in\brick\Lambda_{K}
does. Since
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite, there are only finitely many bricks in
\mods\Lambda
by
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.4]
. Clearly,
M_{K}
has finitely many indecomposable direct summands for all
M\in\mods\Lambda
. If the
N\in\brick\Lambda_{K}
implies
M\in\brick\Lambda
, then the number of bricks in
\Lambda_{K}
is bounded above:

|\brick\Lambda_{K}|\leq(\max\{|B_{K}|:B\in\brick\Lambda\})\cdot|\brick\Lambda|<\infty.

Therefore
\Lambda_{K}
has finitely many isomorphism classes of bricks, hence is
\tau
-tilting finite by
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.4]
.
∎

The last condition imposed in
Lemma

5.5
generally does not hold, as
Example

4.17
and
Example

5.1
show. In the latter example, the indecomposable projective
\Lambda
-module is not a brick, since applying the scalar extension functor gives the sum of indecomposable projective
\Lambda_{K}
modules by
Lemma

1.1
(4). However, these do not form a semibrick, so that the original projective
\Lambda
-module cannot be a brick by
Proposition

3.4
. Nonetheless, each indecomposable projective
\Lambda_{K}
-module is a brick. Moreover, it also does not hold for
Example

5.4
.

Appendix A
Picture groups and faithful group functors over finite fields

by Eric J. Hanson

In this appendix, we discuss the relationship between faithful group functors for
\tau
-cluster morphism categories and (completed, finitary) Ringel–Hall algebras. This expands upon constructions that previously appeared in
[
HI21b
]
and
[
HI21a
]
. The organisation is as follows. In Section
A.1
we recall the definition of the picture group and define a pair of new groups with larger generating sets (the interval-heart groups). In Section
A.2
, we prove that there exists a group functor from the
\tau
-cluster morphism category of an algebra to its interval-heart group and give a sufficient condition for this functor to be faithful. In Section
A.3
, we restrict to algebras over finite fields. We then construct a morphism of groups from the interval-heart group to the group of units of the completed Ringel–Hall algebra and use this to verify the sufficient condition from the previous section. Finally, in Section
A.4
, we discuss the extent to which the results of Section
A.3
extend to algebras over infinite fields. This includes some clarifying remarks on a construction that appeared in
[
HI21a
]
.

A.1.
Picture groups and interval-heart groups

Picture groups were first introduced in
[
ITW16
]
for representation finite hereditary algebras, and the definition has since been generalised in several other works, see e.g.
[
Bø
,
Bør21
,
HI21b
,
IT21
,
Kai24
]
. We use the definition and generality from
[
Kai24
]
. In what follows, we say that a brick
S
is an
f-brick
if
\Filt(S)
is a
\tau
-perpendicular category.

Remark A.1
.

By
[
BH23
, Thm. 1.1]
and Theorem
3.5
,
S
is an f-brick if and only if there exits a left-finite semibrick
\mathcal{S}
with
S\in\mathcal{S}
. We note that the term f-brick is also used in
[
DIJ19
]
for the (generally weaker, see
[
Asa20
, Example 3.13]
) property that
S
itself is a left-finite (semi)brick. Finally, we note that if
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite, then all bricks are f-bricks by
[
DIJ19
, Thms. 4.1 and 4.2]
.

Definition A.2
.

[
Kai24
, Def. 7.2]

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. The
picture group

G(\Lambda)
is defined as having generators

\{X_{S}:S\in\fbrick\Lambda\}\cup\{Y_{\mathcal{T}}:\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda\}

with a relation
Y_{\mathcal{U}}=X_{S}Y_{\mathcal{U}}
whenever there is a cover relation
\mathcal{U}{\,\,<\!\!\!\!\cdot\,\,\,}\mathcal{T}
in
\ftors\Lambda
labelled by
S
, and the relation
Y_{0}=e
.

It will also be useful to consider the following groups.

Definition A.3
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. We define the
interval-heart group
of
\tors\Lambda
, denoted
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
, as having generators

\{Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}:\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\in\tors\Lambda\}

with the following relations:

(1)

For every torsion class
\mathcal{T}\in\tors\Lambda
, there is a relation
Z_{[\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}]}=e
.

(2)

For every
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\in\tors\Lambda
, there is a relation
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{T}]}
.

(3)

For every pair of closed intervals
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
in
\tors\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, there is a relation
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}
.

We define the
interval-heart group
of
\ftors\Lambda
, denoted
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
analogously by replacing each instance of
\tors\Lambda
with
\ftors\Lambda
. To avoid confusion, we distinguish the generators of
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
from those of
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
by writing
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}}\in\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
and
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
.

Remark A.4
.

It is clear from the definitions that the association
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}}\mapsto Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}
extends to a morphism of groups
\iota:\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
. Moreover, by
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.2]
,
\iota
is an isomorphism if
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite.

The picture group is related to the interval-heart group by the following. We implicitly use the fact that every cover relation in
\ftors\Lambda
is also a cover relation in
\tors\Lambda
, which follows from
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.3]
.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Then there is a surjective morphism of groups
\psi:G(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
given on generators as follows.

(1)

For every
\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
, we have
\psi(Y_{\mathcal{T}})=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[0,\mathcal{T}]}
.

(2)

For every
S\in\fbrick\Lambda
, let
\mathcal{U}{\,\,<\!\!\!\!\cdot\,\,\,}\mathcal{T}
be a cover relation in
\ftors\Lambda
labelled by
S
. Then
\psi(X_{S})=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}.

Moreover, if
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite, then
\psi
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We first note that, for any
S\in\fbrick\Lambda
, there exists a cover relation
\mathcal{U}{\,\,<\!\!\!\!\cdot\,\,\,}\mathcal{T}
in
\ftors\Lambda
labelled by
S
. See e.g.
[
Bø
, Lem. 5.2]
for a proof.

Now suppose
\mathcal{U}{\,\,<\!\!\!\!\cdot\,\,\,}\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}{\,\,<\!\!\!\!\cdot\,\,\,}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are both cover relations of
\ftors\Lambda
labelled by the same f-brick
S
. By the definition of the brick labelling (see Section
3.1
), this means
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=\Filt\{S\}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Thus
Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}
, and so
\psi(X_{S})
is well defined. Moreover, one has

\psi(Y_{\mathcal{T}})=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[0,\mathcal{T}]}=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[0,\mathcal{U}]}=\psi(X_{S})\psi(Y_{\mathcal{U}}).

Since also
\psi(Y_{0})=Z_{[0,0]}=e
, we conclude that
\psi
is a morphism of groups. The fact that
\psi
is surjective then follows from observing that

Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}}=Z_{[0,\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}}(Z_{[0,\mathcal{U}]}^{\mathsf{f}})^{-1}=\psi(Y_{\mathcal{T}})\psi((Y_{\mathcal{U}})^{-1})

for any
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
.

Now suppose that
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite, so that
\tors\Lambda=\ftors\Lambda
and
|\ftors\Lambda|<\infty
by
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.2 and Cor. 2.9]
. We define a candidate inverse
\upsilon:\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)\rightarrow G(\Lambda)
by setting
\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}})=Y_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{\mathcal{U}})^{-1}
for every closed interval
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
in
\ftors\Lambda=\tors\Lambda
. There are now three types of relations to consider:

(1)

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
. Then
\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}})=Y_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{\mathcal{T}})^{-1}=e
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
. Then
\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}})\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U}]}^{\mathsf{f}})=Y_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{\mathcal{U}})^{-1}Y_{\mathcal{U}}(Y_{\mathcal{V}})^{-1}=Y_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{\mathcal{V}})^{-1}=\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}})
.

(3)

Let
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
in
\tors\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Now let
(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
be a sequence of bricks labelling a path
\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
in
\mathrm{Hasse}(\ftors\Lambda)
. (Such a path exists since
\ftors\Lambda=\tors\Lambda
is finite.) By Proposition
4.3
, we have that
(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
also labels a path
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
\Lambda
is assumed to be
\tau
-tilting finite, all bricks are f-bricks by Remark
A.1
. Thus we have

\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}^{\mathsf{f}})=Y_{\mathcal{T}}(Y_{\mathcal{U}})^{-1}=X_{S_{1}}\cdots X_{S_{k}}=Y_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}(Y_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}})^{-1}=\upsilon(Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}^{\mathsf{f}}).

We conclude that
\upsilon
is also a morphism of groups. It is then straightforward to verify that
\upsilon
and
\psi
are inverse to one another.
∎

We conclude this section by showing that the group
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
depends only on the abstract lattice structure of
\tors\Lambda
. Note that it is not immediately clear whether the group
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
likewise depends only on the abstract poset structure of
\ftors\Lambda
.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
be finite-dimensional algebras (over possibly distinct fields), and suppose there exists a lattice isomorphism
\eta:\tors\Lambda_{1}\rightarrow\tors\Lambda_{2}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

For any pair of closed intervals
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}],[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}]\subseteq\tors\Lambda_{1}
, one has that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
if and only if
\eta(\mathcal{U})^{\perp}\cap\eta(\mathcal{T})=\eta(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\eta(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.

(2)

There is an isomorphism
\intheart(\tors\Lambda_{1})\rightarrow\intheart(\tors\Lambda_{2})
given on generators by
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}\mapsto Z_{[\eta(\mathcal{U}),\eta(\mathcal{T})]}
.

Proof.

(1) As we now explain, this essentially follows from the proof of
[
Eno23
, Thm. 3.10]
.

In
[
Eno23
, Def. 3.8]
, Enomoto defines a map
\mathsf{j}\text{-}\mathsf{label}
from the set of closed intervals of a completely semidistributive lattice to the power set of the lattice’s set of “completely join-irreducible elements”. It can be verified directly from the definition that this map commutes with lattice isomorphisms. More precisely, in the setup of the theorem, one has that

\{\eta(x):x\in\mathsf{j}\text{-}\mathsf{label}[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]\}=\{\mathsf{j}\text{-}\mathsf{label}[\eta(\mathcal{U}),\eta(\mathcal{T})]\},

and likewise for the primed version of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{T}
. Now the commutative diagram
[
Eno23
, (3.1)]
(found in the proof of
[
Eno23
, Thm. 3.10]
) shows that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathsf{j}\text{-}\mathsf{label}[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]=\mathsf{j}\text{-}\mathsf{label}[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
. The result then follows.

(2) This is an immediate consequence of (1) and the presentation of the interval-heart group.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
A.6
and Proposition
A.5
we obtain the following. Note that this can also be deduced from
[
HI21b
, Thm. 4.10]
and
[
Kai24
, Cor. 1.2]
.

Corollary A.7
.

Let
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
be
\tau
-tilting finite algebras (over possibly distinct fields), and suppose there exists a lattice isomorphism
\eta:\tors\Lambda_{1}\rightarrow\tors\Lambda_{2}
. Then the picture groups
G(\Lambda_{1})
and
G(\Lambda_{2})
are isomorphic.

A.2.
Group functors

Recall that
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
denotes the
\tau
-cluster morphism category of
\Lambda
. Considering the picture group
G(\Lambda)
as a groupoid with one object,
a functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow G(\Lambda)
is constructed in
[
HI21b
, Thm. 4.15]
in the
\tau
-tilting finite case. Furthermore, this functor is shown to be faithful when the picture group
G(\Lambda)
satisfies a technical condition which essentially says that distinct generators of the group must correspond to distinct group elements (see Definition
A.9
below).
In extending this to algebras which are not
\tau
-tilting finite, we find it useful to work with the interval-heart group
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
and the realisation of the
\tau
-cluster morphism category from
[
Kai24
]
that was recalled in Definition
4.4
.

Theorem A.8
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Then

(1)

There is a group functor
\Gamma:\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
given as follows. Let
\rho
be a morphism of
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
, represented by a containment
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
between
\tau
-perpendicular intervals. Then
\Gamma(\rho)=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2}]}
.

(2)

Suppose that, for any closed intervals
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
in
\ftors\Lambda
, we have that
Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}
if and only if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Then the functor
\Gamma
is faithful.

Proof.

(1) We first show that
\Gamma(\rho)
does not depend on the representative of the equivalence class. Suppose that
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\prime}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\prime}]
are representatives of a morphism
\rho
. This means that the domain of
\rho
is
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{2}
and that
[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}=[\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{2}]\cap(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\prime}
, the implication
(1\implies 3
) of Lemma
4.10
then implies that
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}=(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime}
. We conclude that
\Gamma(\rho)
does not depend on the chosen representative of
\rho
.

We now show that
\Gamma
preserves composition. Let
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
be composable morphisms. By Lemma
4.5
, this means there exist
\tau
-perpendicular intervals
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
such that
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
is a representative of
\rho_{1}
,
[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
is a representative of
\rho_{2}
, and
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3}]
is a representative of
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
. We then compute

\Gamma(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{3}]}=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{U}_{3}]}Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2}]}=\Gamma(\rho_{2})\Gamma(\rho_{1}).

(2) Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}:\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
be morphisms in
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda)
with the same domain and codomain. Choose representatives
[\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\prime}]\leq[\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{2}^{\prime}]
of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, respectively.
Similar to the proof of (1), the equivalence
(1\iff 3)
of Lemma
4.10
then implies that
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{2}
. Now by assumption, the second equality holds if and only if
\Gamma(\rho)=\Gamma(\rho^{\prime})
. We conclude that
\Gamma
is faithful.
∎

Motivated by Theorem
A.8
(2) and
[
HI21a
, Thm. 4.15]
, we consider the following definitions.

Definition A.9
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra.

(1)

We say that
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)

has heart-controlled generators
if, for any closed intervals
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
in
\tors\Lambda
, we have that
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}
if and only if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

(2)

We say that
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)

has heart-controlled generators
if, for any closed intervals
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
in
\ftors\Lambda
, we have that
Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z^{\mathsf{f}}_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}
if and only if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

(3)

We say that
G(\Lambda)

has distinct generators
if both of the following hold.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}\in\ftors\Lambda
. Then
Y_{\mathcal{U}}=Y_{\mathcal{T}}\in G(\Lambda)
if and only if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{T}
.

(b)

Let
S,T\in\fbrick\Lambda
. Then
X_{S}=X_{T}\in G(\Lambda)
if and only if
S=T
. Moreover,
X_{S}\neq e\in G(\Lambda)
.

In Section
A.3
, we will show that
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators for any finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
over a finite field. We defer our discussion of those classes of algebras for which
G(\Lambda)
has previously been shown to have distinct generators to Section
A.4
.

Using the definitions of the groups and the morphisms
\iota
(from Remark
A.4
) and
\psi
(from Proposition
A.5
), we obtain the following implications between the three parts of Definition
A.9
.

Proposition A.10
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators then so does
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
.

(2)

If
\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators then
G(\Lambda)
has distinct generators.

We conclude this section by recording the following conjecture. Note that, by Theorem
A.8
and Proposition
A.10
, the faithfulness of
\Gamma
follows from
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
having heart-controlled generators.

Conjecture A.11
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Then
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators and the functor
\Gamma
from Theorem
A.8
is a faithful group functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
.

A.3.
Completed Ringel–Hall algebras

In this section, we prove that the interval-heart groups of finite-dimensional over finite fields have heart-controlled generators. Thus, by Theorem
A.8
, we conclude that the
\tau
-cluster morphism categories of these algebras admit faithful group functors. Our argument uses the machinery of completed (finitary) Ringel–Hall algebras. We introduce these following exposition similar to that of
[
Tre25
, Sec. 7]
.

Fix a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
over a finite field
K
, and fix a skeleton
[\mods\Lambda]
of the category
\mods\Lambda
. For a subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
which is closed under isomorphisms, we denote
[\mathcal{C}]:=\mathcal{C}\cap[\mods\Lambda]
. For
M,N,E\in\mods\Lambda
, we let
c_{M,N}^{E}
denote the number of submodules
M^{\prime}\subseteq E
such that
M^{\prime}\cong N
and
E/M^{\prime}\cong N
. The
completed (finitary) Ringel–Hall algebra
of
\Lambda
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\hall(\Lambda)
whose elements are formal series

\sum_{[M]\in[\mods\Lambda]}a_{[M]}[M]

with multiplication given by

[M][N]=\sum_{[E]\in[\mods\Lambda]}c_{M,N}^{E}[E].

By
[
Rin90
, Prop. 1]
, this is an associative algebra with identity
[0]
.

For a subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
which is closed under isomorphisms, we denote

E_{\mathcal{C}}:=\sum_{[M]\in[\mathcal{C}]}[M]\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda).

The following result of Treffinger forms the basis or our argument.

Proposition A.12
.

[
Tre25
, Thm. 7.1]

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra over a finite field and let

0=\mathcal{T}_{0}\subsetneq\mathcal{T}_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mods\Lambda

be a chain of torsion classes. Then

E_{\mods\Lambda}=E_{\mathcal{T}_{m-1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m}}E_{\mathcal{T}_{m-2}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m-1}}\cdots E_{\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{2}}E_{\mathcal{T}_{0}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}_{1}}.

Before continuing, we establish some additional notation.

•

Let
F\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
. For
M\in\mods\Lambda
, we denote by
F_{[M]}
the coefficient of
M
in
F
. We denote by
\mathsf{supp}(F)
the subcategory of objects
M\in\mods\Lambda
so that
F_{[M]}\neq 0
.

•

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mods_{\ell}\Lambda\subseteq\mods\Lambda
the subcategory consisting of modules of length
\ell
.

•

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathcal{O}(\ell)
the two-sided ideal of
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
generated by the symbols
[M]
for
M\in\mods_{\ell}\Lambda
.

The following lemma is critical.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mods\Lambda
be a subcategory which is closed under isomorphisms. Then
E_{\mathcal{C}}
is invertible in
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
if and only if
0\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
0\in\mathcal{C}
. We symmetrically define two elements
F,F^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
as follows:

(1)

Define
F_{0}=[0]=F^{\prime}_{0}
.

(2)

For
0\neq\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
G_{\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}F_{j}
and
G^{\prime}_{\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}F^{\prime}_{j}
. Then define

F_{\ell}=-\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell})_{[M]}[M],\qquad F^{\prime}_{\ell}=-\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G^{\prime}_{\ell})_{[M]}[M]

(3)

Define
F=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}F_{\ell}
and
F^{\prime}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}F^{\prime}_{\ell}
.

We claim that
E_{\mathcal{C}}F=[0]=F^{\prime}E_{\mathcal{C}}
. In particular, this will imply that
F=F^{\prime}
, and that this is the inverse of
E_{\mathcal{C}}
. We will show only that
E_{\mathcal{C}}F=[0]
, as the proof that
F^{\prime}E_{\mathcal{C}}=[0]
is entirely analogous.

We will demonstrate by induction on
\ell
that
E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell}\in[0]+\mathcal{O}(\ell)
for all
\ell
. Since
E_{\mathcal{C}}(F-G_{\ell})\in\mathcal{O}(\ell)
and
\bigcap_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{O}(\ell)=0
, this will imply the claim. For
\ell=1
, we have
E_{\mathcal{C}}Y_{1}=E_{\mathcal{C}}=[0]+\mathcal{O}(1)
since
0\in\mathcal{C}
. For
\ell>1
, we note that

E_{\mathcal{C}}F_{\ell-1}=-E_{\mathcal{C}}\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell-1}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell-1})_{[M]}[M]\in-\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell-1}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell-1})_{[M]}[M]+\mathcal{O}(\ell)

again because
0\in\mathcal{C}
. By the induction hypothesis, we then have

\displaystyle E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mathcal{C}}(G_{\ell-1}+F_{\ell-1})

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle[0]+\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell-1}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G_{\ell-1})_{[M]}[M]+E_{\mathcal{C}}F_{\ell-1}+\mathcal{O}(\ell)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[0]+\mathcal{O}(\ell).

Now suppose
0\notin\mathcal{C}
. We define an element
F^{\prime\prime}\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
as follows:

(1)

Define
F^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{1}\Lambda]}[M]
.

(2)

For
1<\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}=\sum_{j=m}^{\ell-1}F^{\prime\prime}_{j}
. Then define

F^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}=-\sum_{[M]\in[\mods_{\ell}\Lambda]}(E_{\mathcal{C}}G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})_{[M]}[M].

(3)

Define
F^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}F_{\ell}
.

Similar to the case where
0\in\mathcal{C}
, it can be shown that
E_{\mathcal{C}}G^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{O}(\ell)
for all
\ell>1
. This means
E_{\mathcal{C}}F^{\prime\prime}=0
, and so
E_{\mathcal{C}}
is not invertible.
∎

We now prove the main technical result of this section. We denote by
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)^{*}
the group of units of
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)
.

Proposition A.14
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra over a finite field. Then there is a morphism of groups
\phi:\intheart(\tors\Lambda)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)^{*}
given by
\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})=E_{\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

We note that for every interval
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
in
\tors\Lambda
, we have that
\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})\in\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)^{*}
by Lemma
A.13
. Thus we need only show that
\phi
is well-defined; i.e., that it preserves the relations.

For the first type of relation, let
\mathcal{T}\in\tors\Lambda
. Then
\mathcal{T}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=0
, and so
\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T}]})=e_{\{0\}}=[0]
as desired.

For the second type of relation, let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\in\tors\Lambda
. By Proposition
A.12
, we then have

\displaystyle\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{T},\mods\Lambda]})\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U}]})\phi(Z_{[0,\mathcal{V}]})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mathcal{T}^{\perp}}E_{\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}}E_{\mathcal{V}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{U}}E_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mods\Lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mathcal{T}^{\perp}}E_{\mathcal{V}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}}E_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{T},\mods\Lambda]})\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{T}]})\phi(Z_{[0,\mathcal{V}]}).

By Lemma
A.13
, this implies that
\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{U}]})=\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{V},\mathcal{T}]})
as desired.

Finally, the fact that if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
then
\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})=\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]})
follows immediately from the definition of
\phi
.
∎

Conjecture A.15
.

The morphism
\phi
in Proposition
A.14
is injective.

As a consequence of Proposition
A.14
, we obtain the following.

Theorem A.16
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra over a finite field. Then
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators. Consequently, the functor
\Gamma
from Theorem
A.8
is a faithful group functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]
and
[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]
be closed intervals in
\tors\Lambda
. It is clear from the definition that if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
then
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
. Thus suppose that
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
. Then
E_{\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}}=\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]})=\phi(Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]})=E_{(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(\Lambda)^{*}
by Proposition
A.14
. It follows that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, as desired.

We have shown that
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators. Now the fact that
\Gamma
is a faithful group functor follows from Theorem
A.8
and Proposition
A.10
.
∎

A.4.
Remarks on infinite fields

We conclude this appendix with some remarks on the extent to which Theorem
A.16
can be extended to algebras over infinite fields. Our starting point is the following.

Corollary A.17
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finite-dimensional algebra over an arbitrary field, and suppose there exists a lattice isomorphism
\eta:\tors\Lambda\rightarrow\tors\Lambda^{\prime}
for some finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda^{\prime}
over a finite field. Then
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators. Consequently, the functor
\Gamma
from Theorem
A.8
is a faithful group functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}],[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}]
be closed intervals in
\tors\Lambda
. It is clear than if
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
then
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
. Thus suppose that
Z_{[\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T}]}=Z_{[\mathcal{U}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
. Then, by Lemma
A.6
(2), we have
Z_{[\eta(\mathcal{U}),\eta(\mathcal{T})]}=Z_{[\eta(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}),\eta(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})]}\in\intheart(\tors\Lambda_{1})
. Theorem
A.16
then implies that
\eta(\mathcal{U})^{\perp}\cap\eta(\mathcal{T})=\eta(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\eta(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
. By Lemma
A.6
(1), we conclude that
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

We have shown that
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators. Now the fact that
\Gamma
is a faithful group functor follows from Theorem
A.8
and Proposition
A.10
.
∎

Remark A.18
.

Suppose that
\Lambda
is
\tau
-tilting finite in the setup of Corollary
A.17
. Then the fact that
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)
admits a faithful group functor can also be deduced from
[
Kai24
, Sec. 7]
.

We now give several examples demonstrating how one can use Corollary
A.17
in practice. Note that the (
\tau
-)cluster morphism categories of representation finite (and also tame) hereditary algebras were previously shown to admit faithful group functors in
[
IT22
, Thm. 3.7]
.

Proposition A.19
.

Let
\Lambda
be a representation finite hereditary algebra over an arbitrary field
K
, and let
\Pi(\Lambda)
denote the preprojective algebra of
\Lambda
. Then
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
and
\intheart(\tors\Pi(\Lambda))
both have heart-controlled generators. Consequently, the functor
\Gamma
from Theorem
A.8
is a faithful group functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
(resp.
\mathfrak{W}(\Pi(\Lambda))\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Pi(\Lambda))
).

Proof.

By
[
DR75
, Thm. C]
, we have that
\Lambda
is Morita equivalent to the tensor algebra
K\mathbb{S}
of a Dynkin
K
-species
\mathbb{S}
. The preprojective algebras
\Pi(\Lambda)
and
\Pi(K\mathbb{S})
are then also Morita equivalent by
[
CB99
, Thm. 2.3 and Cor. 5.5]
. Now let
K^{\prime}
be a finite field such that there exists a
K^{\prime}
-species
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
with the same underlying modulated quiver as
\mathbb{S}
.

In the simply-laced case, it follows from
[
IT09
, Thm. 4.3]
that
\tors(K\mathbb{S})(\cong\tors\Lambda)
and
\tors(K^{\prime}\mathbb{S}^{\prime})
are both isomorphic to the
Cambrian lattice
determined by the (modulated) quiver underlying
\mathbb{S}
. The analogous result can also be deduced outside the simply-laced case by combining
[
GLS20
, Thm. 1]
and
[
Gyo22
, Cor. 8.17]
.

Similarly,
[
AHI+22
, Thm. 7.9]
and Theorem
2.10
imply that
\ftors(\Pi(K\mathbb{S}))(\cong\ftors(\Pi(\Lambda)))
and
\ftors(\Pi(K^{\prime}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}))
are both anti-isomorphic to the
weak order
on the Coxeter group determined by the modulated graph underlying
\mathbb{S}
. Now since
\mathbb{S}
is Dynkin, it is well-known that this Coxeter group is finite. Thus
\Pi(\Lambda)
and
\Pi(K^{\prime}\mathbb{S}^{\prime})
are
\tau
-tilting finite and, by
[
DIJ19
, Thm. 1.2]
, one has

\tors(\Pi(\Lambda))=\ftors(\Pi(\Lambda))\cong\ftors(\Pi(K^{\prime}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}))=\tors(\Pi(K^{\prime}\mathbb{S}^{\prime})).

Corollary
A.17
now implies the result.
∎

For the next example, we refer to
[
BR86
]
for the definition of a string algebra and for results concerning string and band modules that we apply without proof.

Proposition A.20
.

Let
\Lambda
be a representation finite string algebra over an arbitrary field
K
. Then
\intheart(\tors\Lambda)
has heart-controlled generators. Consequently, the functor
\Gamma
from Theorem
A.8
is a faithful group functor
\mathfrak{W}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\intheart(\ftors\Lambda)
.

Proof.

By the definition of a string algebra, there exists a quiver
Q
and a set of paths
p
in
Q
such that
\Lambda=KQ/(p)
. The definition then implies that
K^{\prime}Q/(p)
is also a string algebra for any field
K^{\prime}
. In particular, we can take
K^{\prime}
to be finite.

The indecomposable modules over a string algebra are classified (up to isomorphism) as “string modules” and “band modules”. It is well known that a string algebra is representation infinite if and only if it admits a band module. Moreover, while the classification of band modules depends on the field, the existence of a band module depends only of
Q
and
p
. Thus
K^{\prime}Q/(p)
is also representation finite and all indecomposable modules (over either
\Lambda=KQ/(p)
or
K^{\prime}Q/(p)
) are string modules.

Unlike band modules, the classification of string modules depends only on
Q
and
p
, not on the choice of base field. The same is also true for the dimension of the Hom-space between string modules by a result of
[
CB89
]
. Putting this together, we conclude that there exists a bijection
\omega:\mathrm{ind}(\mods\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathrm{ind}(\mods K^{\prime}Q/(p))
such that
\dim_{K}\Hom_{\Lambda}(X,Y)=\dim_{K^{\prime}}\Hom_{K^{\prime}Q/(p)}(\omega(X),\omega(Y))
for all
X
and
Y
.

Now it is well known that a subcategory
\mathcal{T}
is a torsion class if and only if
\mathcal{T}={}^{\perp}(\mathcal{T}^{\perp})
. Thus
\omega
extends to an isomorphism
\tors(\Lambda)\rightarrow\tors(K^{\prime}Q/(p))
. Corollary
A.17
then implies the result.
∎

Special cases of representation finite string algebras include
\tau
-tilting finite gentle algebras (see
[
Pla19
, Thm. 1.1]
or
[
Mou23
, Thm. 7.1]
) and the “Nakayama-like algebras” studied in
[
HI21a
]
. Faithful group functors for these classes of algebras were previously established in
[
HI21a
, Sec. 5]
. We conclude with some clarifying comments about this result.

In
[
HI21a
, Sec. 5.2]
, we introduced what we called the “power series 0-Hall algebra” of a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
(over a field
K
). We then used this to show that the picture groups of Nakayama-like algebras and of
\tau
-tilting finite gentle algebras whose quivers have no loops or 2-cycles have distinct generators. (We then used
[
HI21a
, Thm. 4.15]
to conclude that the
\tau
-cluster morphism categories of these algebras admit faithful group functors.) Implicit in the construction of the power series 0-Hall algebra are the assumptions that (1) if
K
is finite then
\Lambda
admits Hall polynomials, and (2) if
K
is infinite then one can replace
K
with a finite field and use the resulting algebra to understand the representation theory of
\Lambda
. Assumption (2) can be made precise by, for example, requiring the existence of a bijection
\eta
as in the proof of Proposition
A.20
. In particular, this means assumption (2) is satisfied by
\tau
-tilting finite gentle algebras and by Nakayama-like algebras. On the other hand, the problem of determining whether the algebras in these families satisfy assumption (1) seems to be open, see e.g.
[
CY24
]
for some work in this direction. To avoid this existence problem, one could instead use Proposition
A.20
to deduce
[
HI21a
, Cor. 5.13]
.

Finally, we recall that picture groups of Nakayama algebras were shown to have distinct generators in
[
HI21b
, Sec. 4]
using a different, but morally related, construction. While this construction does not rely on the existence of Hall polynomials (and is independent of the base field), we note that Nakayama algebras over finite fields are known to admit Hall polynomials
[
NI10
]
.
